| D-64 | A **mirrorable** panel needs no presenter code at all | locked | The presenter is a **second Excalidraw instance** (`excalidraw-app/presenter/PresenterView.tsx:539-558`), so anything the library mounts is mounted there too. The GIF proved it: the animated-image overlay is mounted in the **library** (`packages/excalidraw/components/App.tsx:2453-2473`), and the presenter renders the animation with **zero** presenter-specific code — the only thing the app had to add was moving the bytes. So a panel whose content is **in the document's own file store** (a video file, a flow document) is mirrorable for free. Only a **bridge-backed** panel — whose content lives in the VS Code process — needs presenter-side work, and that is limited to a **read-only** mirror (the owner's decision, question 8). |
| D-65 | Only the **poster** crosses the window boundary | locked | The presenter's transport has **no size guard** on `presenter:files` (`excalidraw-app/presenter/usePresenterBroadcaster.ts:168-194`): the editor replies with the complete `BinaryFileData`, `dataURL` and all, so a 40 MB video posted there would either block or be dropped with a console error. The rule is therefore that a panel's **bytes never travel the files channel** — only its **poster** does, and a poster is an ordinary PNG in the existing store. The panel's _source_ is referenced by id, exactly as an image's is. A size guard is added to the files channel as well, so a future change cannot turn this into an accident. |
| D-66 | `BinaryFileData["mimeType"]` is widened to admit video | locked | The restriction is in `packages/excalidraw/types.ts:207-211`, not where the presenter design cited. It already admits `application/octet-stream` through the `\| typeof MIME_TYPES.binary` escape hatch, and **nothing validates the mime at runtime** — `insertIntoScene.ts:288` does an unchecked cast — so this is a **type-level** widening, not the removal of a guard. A `VIDEO_MIME_TYPES` map is added beside `IMAGE_MIME_TYPES` in `packages/common/src/constants.ts:237`, spread into `MIME_TYPES`, and the union widened. A video can then live in the document's own file store. |
| D-67 | The bridge is discovered by a **fixed loopback port** plus an **in-band token** | locked | Nothing in the repository discovers a local helper process by scanning ports, and `BroadcastChannel` cannot reach a Node process (it is same-origin, browser-tabs-only). The collaboration server is the precedent: a **fixed build-time port** from a `VITE_APP_*` variable (`VITE_APP_WS_SERVER_URL`, `.env.development:12`) with **no token**. The bridge does the same, with the **per-session token negotiated in the first WebSocket frame** rather than a second discovery channel. The app connects to one known port and authenticates in band; it never scans, and the token is never on the command line or in a URL. |
| D-68 | Cropping a poster is **app-level new UI** | locked | The library's existing crop is an **in-place crop mode on an image element** (`croppingElementId` in `AppState`, the handlers at `packages/excalidraw/components/App.tsx:10512` and `:13013`, and the `crop` field on `ExcalidrawImageElement`). It is **not** a modal, and it does **not** produce a new element. The design's "crop a poster into a **new** image element, leaving the panel untouched" therefore has **no existing implementation** — it is new UI, and it lives in `excalidraw-app/components/`. It cannot use the library's `Modal`, which is bound to the app's `openDialog` state and cannot carry a promise (D-15). |
| D-69 | the gate runs its read-only steps in parallel and sizes vitest from the free memory | locked | The gate's four steps are **not** equally heavy. `tsc`, `eslint` and `prettier` only **read** files and do not depend on each other, so running them one after another wastes the machine: measured on 2026-10-21, they took 32.4 s, 89.2 s and 6.8 s — 128 s of the 374 s the whole gate took. They now **start together** and are reported in the same order, with the same format and the same 30-minute timeout each. `vitest` still runs **after** them, because it is the step that writes and the one that needs the machine's memory to itself; its worker count is now derived from the machine — `max(1, min(floor(cpuThreads / 2), floor((freeMB - 1500) / 600)))` — and passed as `vitest run --maxWorkers=<n> --minWorkers=1`, with `GATE_JOBS` overriding it (`GATE_JOBS=1` gives vitest a single worker, the old one-at-a-time behaviour, for diagnosing a failure that only appears in parallel). A test that **measures time or speed** is run on its own afterwards, with a single worker, so its measurement is not taken while the machine is busy. The gate **refuses to start** while another gate or test run is in flight, and waits for the machine to go quiet instead of letting two runs fight for memory — which is how the OS ends up killing vitest's workers (the fourth fault in the HANDOVER's flake account). Nothing is weakened: the same commands, the same thresholds, the same TAP summary, and the same step count. The one summary line — workers used, free memory before vitest, total minutes — is printed **and** written to `.tins/state-gate-summary.txt`, because the kit shows only PASS or FAIL. |
| D-31 | dagre lays the flowchart out, @types/dagre types it | locked | The flowchart's **auto-layout** uses `dagre` (`0.8.5`), added as a runtime dependency of `packages/excalidraw`, with `@types/dagre` (`0.7.54`) for its types. The owner's approval, 2026-10-21. The reason: the animation's step order comes from the arrows, and a layout that is not the author's own would draw arrows between nodes that are not neighbours, so the order would be the layout's rather than the drawing's. These are the **only** dependencies the fork adds to the pre-existing toolchain that D-100 locks. They are used for the layout step only — the animation, the overlay and the reading order do not depend on them. The kit's dependency lint reads the **root** `package.json` only, so it does not see a workspace package's dependencies; this row is the record the lint cannot make. |
| D-32 | pyodide is the in-browser fallback, downloaded lazily | locked | The code panel's **graceful degradation** — the path that runs Python when the VS Code bridge is not connected — is **Pyodide**, and it is **downloaded lazily at run time**: the runtime is fetched only when the user asks for it, never on page load. The owner's approval, 2026-10-21 ("lazy download pyodide"). It is deliberately **not** an npm dependency: an installed Pyodide is ~10 MB of the repository's own weight and a 10 MB download on a slow classroom connection, while a lazily fetched runtime is paid for only by the teachers who use it. The panel's fallback is therefore reachable with no installation at all, and the panel itself says so plainly when the bridge is absent. |
| D-74 | an element the host app adds carries a fractional index before the scene sees it | locked | Every element is born with `index: null` - that is what a null index **means** ("not yet assigned to the scene", `packages/element/src/types.ts:69`). The library assigns the index itself when **it** inserts an element: `Scene.insertElementsAtIndex` calls `syncMovedIndices` **before** `replaceAllElements` (`packages/element/src/Scene.ts:367-369`). But the scene **validates** the indices before it syncs them (`Scene.ts:281-285`), and a null index fails that validation (`isValidFractionalIndex` returns false for a missing index, `packages/element/src/fractionalIndex.ts:384`) - and in dev and test the validation **throws**. A host app that adds an element through `updateScene` therefore has to sync the indices itself, with the library's own `syncInvalidIndices`. Without this, the **first** element a teacher adds to an empty canvas dies with `InvalidFractionalIndexError`: the validator is throttled to one call per sixty minutes with a leading edge, so the first `replaceAllElements` after the page loads is the one that throws, and every insert after it happens to work - which is why the bug looked random. The rule is therefore: **every place where the app adds an element syncs the indices first** (`excalidraw-app/bridge/insertCodePanel.ts` and the poster crop in `excalidraw-app/App.tsx`). The tripwire is not weakened: the library still validates every element set it is given, and the app still hands it elements that satisfy the validation. |
| D-75 | the panel says what it knows, and the extension always says which editor state it has | locked | A panel that is connected but shows **nothing** is indistinguishable from a broken panel. The rule is that neither side may be silent about a state it knows: the **extension** publishes the editor state **even when no file is open** - with an empty uri - so the panel can tell "no file is open in the editor" apart from "the extension has not answered yet"; and the **panel** renders that difference instead of a blank region (`labels.panel.code.noFile` existed since the wave 5b wave but was never rendered). The same rule applies to the **console**: the connection line is a status, not a log entry, and the console rides in the element's payload, which is saved with the document - so the latest connection line **replaces** the previous one instead of adding to it, and the console cannot grow for ever. And the extension does not publish on **every selection change**: a cursor move fires it, and the panel would then write the whole scene again for every move of the mouse; the panel mirrors the **text**, not the caret, so a text change and a change of the active file are the only two things it needs. |
| D-76 | the bridge says which editor it is | locked | The bridge's port is **fixed** (D-67), so with two editors running only one of them holds it - and the app reaches that one, not necessarily the one the teacher has their file open in. Measured on 2026-10-21: the listening socket on 47511 belonged to **VSCodium**, while the teacher's file was open in **VS Code**; the panel connected (the token matched, because the token was copied from the same instance) and stayed empty, and nothing in the protocol said the two were different editors. The `bridge:hello` therefore carries the editor's name (`vscode.env.appName`), and the app shows it in the panel's chrome and in the console line it already writes. The name is **optional** in the protocol guard, so an extension that predates the field still connects - it simply does not say which editor it is. The mirror itself needed no change: proven with a controlled fake bridge, the panel renders the editor's text with CodeMirror's line numbers the moment the extension publishes it. |
| D-77 | losing the port is loud and actionable | locked | The editor that loses the race for the fixed port used to say only _"could not listen on port 47511"_ in a toast that appears once and is easy to miss - and the teacher had no way of connecting that to the empty panel. The message now names the port, says that another editor has it, and gives the two ways out: close that editor, or give this one a different port (`excalidraw-bridge.port`) and point the app at it (`VITE_APP_BRIDGE_URL`). A failure that is **not** the port says what the failure was and does not blame another editor. The rule lives in `packages/vscode-bridge/extension/src/portConflict.ts`, which imports nothing from `vscode`, so it is testable without an editor. |
| D-78 | the mirror follows the file, not the focus | locked | The panel mirrors **the file the teacher is teaching from**, not whatever has the keyboard focus. The API says it plainly: `vscode.window.activeTextEditor` is `undefined` far more often than it looks - the focus moves to the terminal (the extension runs the program in one), to the explorer, to the extensions view - and `onDidChangeActiveTextEditor` **also fires when the active editor changes to `undefined`** (the installed declaration says so verbatim). So the old code published the empty state in that moment, and **erased the mirror**: the code the panel was showing vanished, for a reason the teacher could neither see nor undo. The extension therefore **remembers** the last editor (`editorToMirror` in `packages/vscode-bridge/extension/src/editorState.ts`) and keeps publishing it for as long as its document is open; only a document that is actually **closed** clears it. The rule is a pure function, so it is testable without an editor. |
| D-79 | the active tab names the file, and it is resolved by uri | locked | The panel mirrors **the file the teacher is teaching from**, and the tab is what says which file that is. `vscode.window.activeTextEditor` is the editor that **has focus**, or - when nothing has focus - the one that **changed input most recently** (the installed API declaration says so verbatim). So it is the wrong thing to mirror: the teacher can be looking at file A while the last input was in file B, and the moment a terminal or the Explorer takes the focus, `activeTextEditor` is `undefined` even though the file is still open in a tab. The **active tab** is the right thing, and the tab is known whether or not the window has the focus. The tab API does **not** carry the document: `TabInputText` has only a `uri` (verified against the installed declaration, VSCodium 1.135.06055), so the document is resolved from the editor's list of **open documents** by that uri - and a tab that names no text document (a terminal, a notebook, a webview) resolves to nothing. This row was first written against a stub that had a `document` on the tab input, which does not exist; the stub has now been taken from the real declaration and the rule corrected. The fallback order is: the active **editor**, then the active **tab's** document, then the last editor whose document is still open. |
| D-39 | one scan, and a reason for everything it cannot use | locked | The sidebar had **two** scanners and **two** accept lists: `scanFolder` kept the images and threw the rest away, `scanDocuments` kept the pdf/markdown/code files and threw the rest away, and `DOCUMENT_ACCEPT` had drifted from `classify` — `.rb .kt .rs .dart .ex .hs .zig .ps1 .ini .env .gitignore` were classified as code but were absent from the accept list. There is now **one** entry point, `scanFiles` in `excalidraw-app/sidebar/sidebarFiles.ts`, and the accept list (`FILE_ACCEPT`) is **derived from the classifier**: the classifier is the authority, and the two cannot drift apart again. The scan returns the usable files **and** the unusable ones, each with a reason (`video-not-embeddable`, `unknown-type`, `read-failed`), so nothing is dropped on the way to the list. The kinds are `image`, `gif`, `video`, `pdf`, `markdown`, `code`, `text` — a GIF is a kind of its own because it animates, and plain text is a kind of its own because it is neither markdown nor source code. |
| D-40 | the list is grouped by type, and the badge is per kind | locked | The list is grouped by type in a **fixed order** — Images, Documents, Markdown, Code, Video — each group with an icon of its own; the files that cannot be used are grouped last ("Unsupported") and each one is **named with the reason** (D-39). The badge on a row carries the label of **its own kind** — IMG, GIF, PDF, MD, CODE, TXT, VID — because the old badge had one label for everything that was neither a PDF nor a markdown file, so a `.ts` file was labelled "TXT"; and the class `app-doc-badge--code` was emitted but had **no CSS at all**. Every badge class now has a colour of its own, and the group header carries the icon that names the type. |
| D-41 | the picker takes a file or a folder, and the pin keeps your place in it | locked | The one picker has **two** inputs — one for files, one for a folder (`webkitdirectory`) — because the owner's item 9 asks for "select file and select folder only". A folder pick is a **flat** list whose files carry their relative paths, so the **whole root is kept** (`excalidraw-app/sidebar/browserState.ts`) and the browsing path (`cwd`) is a directory of that root: a folder can be walked into, and the back button returns to the parent listing. The **pin's** remaining job is the browsing path — the picked list and the active tab already survive a page switch (the sidebar does not unmount, and the active tab is the persisted `appState.openSidebar.tab`) — so what has to be kept is the folder the user had walked into, and it lives **outside the panel**, so it survives the panel being re-created too. The arm and the placement are shared: `armInsert` and `placementFor` in `insertIntoScene.ts`, with the image arm still a canvas `pointerdown` listener, the code arm still the library's paste hook (Wave 4a, D-18), and the drag arm still image-only with the same `DRAG_MIME`. |
| D-45 | one panel element, one overlay, and the per-kind waves only say what is **inside** | locked | The three panels — a mirrored editor, a video with captions, an animated flowchart — have nothing in common except that they are **live surfaces on the canvas**, so the machinery is built **once** and the per-kind waves describe only the content. The machinery is: the `panel` element type (`packages/element/src/types.ts`), the DOM overlay that mounts a live panel (`packages/excalidraw/components/PanelOverlay.tsx`), the lock transition that freezes a panel into a poster (`packages/excalidraw/scene/lockPanel.ts`), the crop that turns a poster into a **new** image element (`excalidraw-app/components/cropModal.tsx`, D-68), and the OCR hook (`excalidraw-app/ocrClient.ts`). The element carries its **kind** (`panelKind`) and its **payload** (`panel`), and the overlay dispatches on the kind; the per-kind wave replaces the surface and nothing else. The alternative — a separate element type per kind — would have multiplied the 130 dispatch points of the type checklist by three, and TypeScript does not protect those points (`packages/tsconfig.base.json` sets `strict` but not `noImplicitReturns`, so a switch with a `default` arm fails **silently**). |
| D-46 | a panel's live state rides inside the element, and the payload is **plain** data | locked | A panel's live state is **in** the element (`panel`), not beside it: the element list is the only channel the document has, and a side channel would not survive a save, a reload or the presenter's wholesale `replaceAllElements`. The transport is `postMessage`, so the payload must be **plain, structured-cloneable data**: a `<video>` element, a function or a class instance in it makes the postMessage throw, and the frame is then **silently dropped**. The payload's type says so — `PanelInlineValue` is a JSON-shaped union, and `PanelSource` names only a file id, a bridge session id or an inline value. The poster's bytes do **not** ride in the payload either: a poster is an ordinary PNG in the document's own file store, referenced by `posterFileId` (D-65). |
| D-47 | a panel is **floating**, and the lock transition is **reversible** | locked | A panel is `floating: true`, and it is set in **one** place — the panel's own factory (`newPanelElement`) — because the flag is opt-in and is never set automatically, and its nine read sites already mean exactly what a panel needs: "not owned by its page, may sit outside the page rect, and widens the camera clamp". So a panel needs **no** new page-model mechanism, and it must never become a frame child (`isEligibleFrameChildType("panel")` is `false`). The lock transition is reversible because the live payload is **never discarded**: locking sets `posterMode` and `posterFileId`, unlocking clears them, and the panel's `panel` payload is the same object on both sides. A poster is captured at the content's **native** resolution, not at the size the panel happens to be shown at. |
| D-50 | the bridge is on the loopback interface, on a **fixed** port, and the token is negotiated **in band** | locked | The bridge listens on **127.0.0.1 only**, on a **fixed port** (47511 by default) that the app learns from the build-time `VITE_APP_BRIDGE_URL` — the same shape as `VITE_APP_WS_SERVER_URL`, the collaboration server's URL (D-67). The WebSocket handshake must present a **per-session token** and a **localhost origin**; anything else is rejected. The token travels in the **first frame** (`bridge:auth`) and **never** in a URL — a URL ends up in the browser's history, in a proxy log and in a `Referer` header — and **never** on a command line. The app's side is `excalidraw-app/bridge/bridgeSession.ts`, a thin wrapper around the **shared** client `packages/vscode-bridge/bridge/src/client.ts`: there is no second client and no second copy of the protocol. |
| D-51 | the extension **runs** the program; the panel mirrors what it says | locked | The extension runs the program itself, through a contributed task type (`excalidraw-bridge.run`) and a `Pseudoterminal`, because VS Code's stable API cannot read an arbitrary terminal's output. The teacher's own terminal still shows the output normally. The panel is the **mirror**, and it has three regions: the **editor** (the mirrored text), the **output** (the program's stdout/stderr) and the **console** (the bridge's own messages). The live state rides inside the element's payload as **plain data** (D-46), so it survives a save, a reload and the presenter's `replaceAllElements`; the payload's width is capped, because the payload is saved with the document. The panel's content is CodeMirror 6, reusing the library's own `CodeMirrorEditor` as the base component. |
| D-52 | the panel is **read-only**, and a missing bridge is an **explicit** state | locked | The panel is a mirror: the teacher's VS Code editor is the source of truth, so the panel's CodeMirror is mounted with `EditorState.readOnly` and `EditorView.editable` off, and it does **not** take the keyboard focus — a live panel that swallowed every keystroke would make the editor unusable while it is on the screen. A blocked clipboard or a missing bridge shows an **explicit state** — **not connected**, **connected** or **rejected** — and never a blank editor, because a blank editor is indistinguishable from a program that produced nothing. The token is held **in memory for the session only**: never in `localStorage`, never in a URL, never in the document (the panel's payload is saved with it), and the field masks it. The token reaches the app the way the owner chose: the teacher copies it in the editor and pastes it into the panel's **Bridge** field, which calls `navigator.clipboard.readText()`. |
| D-53 | the captions live in an ordinary text element **beside** the panel, and no caption means no element | locked | The owner's ask (2026-10-21): the captions should if possible not be inside the video player but outside of it, in the text box Excalidraw already has, which gets refreshed as the video plays — and if there is no caption, no such option. So the caption is an **ordinary Excalidraw text element** beside the panel: it is edited, restyled, selected and undone like any other text, and it is never burnt into the picture. The panel's own `<track>` elements carry the cues (the browser reads them) but **none of them is `default`**, so the player does not draw the caption. A video with **no** caption track gets **no** caption element at all — not an empty text box, not a placeholder — and the rule is the element factory's: no track, no element. The write goes through the ordinary element machinery, so it goes through **undo**; a cue changes a few times a minute, so this is not a hot path. |
| D-54 | the caption **binding** is scene data on the panel element, and not in the payload | locked | The binding names the text element, the panel and the track's language, so the caption survives a page switch, a reload and the presenter (which is a second Excalidraw instance that gets the document and nothing else). It points at **another** element, so it is not the panel's content: the payload is the panel's own content and travels as plain data (D-46), while the binding is a **relationship between two elements** of the document. It therefore lives on the panel **element**, beside the payload, next to `posterMode` and `posterFileId` — the facts the foundation already keeps on the element rather than in the payload. The element type lives in `@excalidraw/element`, outside this wave's scope, so the widening happens in **one** place (`CaptionBoundPanel` in `packages/excalidraw/scene/panelContent.ts`) and every reader goes through `readCaptionBinding()`. The **track** itself (the cues) is the panel's own content and rides in the payload's `options.captions` — the home the foundation already defines. |
| D-55 | a video's poster is captured at the video's **own** size, and the OCR request carries the **region** | locked | The poster rule of the foundation (D-47) is the content's native size; for a video that is the video's **own** size (`videoWidth` × `videoHeight`) and never the size the panel happens to be shown at on the canvas — a poster taken from the on-canvas size is a thumbnail, and a thumbnail is not a poster. The capture draws the frame into a canvas of that size, and the panel's `naturalWidth`/`naturalHeight` are set to it. The OCR request carries a **region** — the part of the image to read, in the image's **own pixels** — because the ROI crop is the one OCR lever that costs nothing and buys the most, and the service can crop before it reads. The budget is a **gate**, not a hope: a full 1080p frame under 1.5 s and a subtitle-band crop under 250 ms, measured **on the machine** with the worker created once, the language data cached and the core not pinned to a single `.js` file (so the loader can pick the SIMD core). The numbers — median and p95, both cases — are recorded in `excalidraw-app/ocr/benchmark-result.json`. |
| D-80 | the code panel wears the **VS Code look**, and it is half the canvas | locked | The panel mirrors the editor's **text** and the program's output, and it **cannot** be a pixel mirror of the VS Code window: the extension API that VSCodium and VS Code ship (`resources/app/out/vscode-dts/vscode.d.ts`, 763 KB) has **no** screenshot, capture-image or canvas-export facility of any kind - an extension gets the document, the text and the selection, never the rendered pixels. A literal window view in the canvas is a **screencast**, and that is the video panel's job (wave 5c). What the panel can be is the editor's **look** and the editor's **size** (D-51 stays locked). The **name**: the panel says WHICH file it is mirroring, on a tab strip, the way a VS Code tab says it - the file's name and its language. The bridge already sends the uri and the language (D-51, D-78), so the protocol does not change: the panel derives the name from the uri's **last segment** (`codePanelFileName`; `file:///home/sai/main.py` is `main.py`). With no file open the tab strip says so, with the wording the panel already uses for that case (`labels.panel.code.noFile`). The **theme**: the VS Code dark theme - #1e1e1e background, #d4d4d4 text, and the VS Code token colours (keywords #c586c0, control flow #c586c0, functions #dcdcaa, strings #ce9178, comments #6a9955, variables #9cdcfe - the palette is corrected to the **semantic** one by D-81) - **whatever the app's theme is**, because the teacher is looking at VS Code while they teach. The palette is plain data in one place (`components/vscodeTheme.ts`), and the panel's own surface, its tab strip and the editor all read it from there; the **code card** - the thing that gets pasted - keeps its own colours, because it is a different surface. The **size**: a **share of the visible canvas**, not a constant. `codePanelSize(viewport, zoom)` is half the viewport, with the zoom divided out so the panel covers half the _screen_ at any zoom, clamped to 280-1200 x 200-1200 scene units; the owner's own numbers fall out of it (990 x 1024 on a 1980 x 2048 screen, where the old fixed 520 x 380 was a fifth of it). The **code region keeps its height**: the chrome, the output and the console must not squeeze the code out of the panel, so the editor region has a floor (120px) and the output and the console shrink (and scroll) instead. That contract is inline, because it is a rule and not a look. |
| D-81 | the panel wears the colours VS Code **paints**, and the bridge's task never asks for the focus | locked | Two corrections, both found by reading the VS Code and VSCodium sources (VS Code 1.144.0, commit 1519239), and both **corrections** rather than preferences. **The palette**: it was written from memory of the classic Dark+ theme, and the **semantic** token colours - the ones VS Code actually paints, because semantic highlighting is on - differ from the TextMate scope colours. The most visible error: VS Code paints a keyword **#C586C0** (purple), not #569cd6 (blue); #569cd6 is the colour of the bare TextMate scope `keyword`, which the semantic keyword token does not even probe (it probes `keyword.control`). So in a real VS Code, `import`, `def`, `return` and `for` are purple. The palette is corrected from `extensions/theme-defaults/themes/dark_plus.json` (the `semanticTokenColors`, and the chrome in `colors`; Dark+ and Dark Modern share the SAME token palette and differ only in the chrome) and `extensions/theme-defaults/themes/dark_vs.json` (the TextMate scopes, for the tokens semantic highlighting does not cover): keyword and control flow #C586C0, comment #6A9955, string #ce9178, function/method #DCDCAA, type/class/namespace #4EC9B0, variable/parameter #9CDCFE, number #b5cea8, operator #d4d4d4, regexp #646695, constant/enum member #4FC1FF, tag and storage/type keyword #569cd6; and the chrome: editor background and gutter #1E1E1E, default text #D4D4D4, line number #858585, active line number #c6c6c6, cursor #AEAFAD, selection #264F78. The palette stays plain data in one place (`components/vscodeTheme.ts`) with **no imports** - the editor is heavy and the panel loads it lazily. **The current line** is a **2px solid #282828 border**, not a tinted band: neither Dark+ nor Dark Modern defines `editor.lineHighlightBackground`, so VS Code draws an outline (`src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/currentLineHighlight/`: `border: 2px solid <editor.lineHighlightBorder>`) - the panel draws the border instead of the band it drew. **The panel stays a RECONSTRUCTION**: it renders the editor itself, in the app, in the browser, because no extension API - stable, proposed or internal - can return the editor's DOM node, a bitmap or the rendered view (the extension host is a DOM-less Node process, the message bridge is JSON-only, and a repo-wide sweep for `copyImage` / `saveImage` / `exportImage` / `captureScreenshot` / `getEditorDomNode` finds **zero** matches). **The task**: the task `excalidraw-bridge.run` runs the program in a pseudoterminal, and a pseudoterminal is a terminal - where it lands comes from `terminal.integrated.defaultLocation`, whose default is `view` (the terminal panel), in which case the editor keeps the focus and `vscode.window.activeTextEditor` is untouched. When that setting is `editor`, the terminal becomes an editor in the editor area, the active editor becomes the terminal, and `activeTextEditor` becomes `undefined` - exactly the moment the mirror used to blank (D-78). So the task carries presentation options that **reveal the terminal and never ask for the focus**: `reveal: Always`, `focus: false` (in VS Code's own task system, `revealTerminal` happens on its own and `focusInstance` happens only when the presentation asks for the focus). The rule lives in `packages/vscode-bridge/extension/src/taskPresentation.ts`, which imports nothing from `vscode` and is therefore testable without an editor. The teacher's own task, the one their `tasks.json` contributes, keeps their presentation. |
| D-56 | the playback position rides on the **panel element**, in seconds | locked | The position is **shared state**: the presenter is a second Excalidraw instance and it has to show the same video at the same place (D-57). It rides on the **element**, beside the payload - the same way the caption binding does (D-54) - so it travels with the scene and there is **no second transport** for it. The editor writes it on a **coarse tick** (about 4 Hz): the scene message is throttled to 40 ms and carries the whole element list, so a write per frame would flood the channel for nothing. The **presenter applies** it and interpolates locally between the updates (`playbackRate`, and `requestVideoFrameCallback` where the browser has it): a small drift is closed by the rate, a large one by a seek. The presenter **never writes it back** - a view-only host does not write - because two windows writing to each other would fight over the same video. |
| D-57 | the presenter's panels come from the scene it already carries | locked | The presenter is a **second Excalidraw instance** and it mounts the **same** panel overlay the editor does, from the **same** element list - so a read-only mirror of a panel needs **no new transport** (D-64). A **locked** panel is an ordinary image element with a poster, and a code card is ordinary text elements: both travel as they always did. Only a **live** panel's content needs a transport of its own, and that is out of this wave. The presenter's code panel is **read-only**, as the owner decided (question 8, 2026-10-21): the interactive mode - a `presenter:input` message, an editor-side handler, a presenter-side post path - is a meaningfully larger surface (input focus, cursor position, IME, undo) and is **not** in this wave. |
| D-58 | a video's bytes travel the **existing** files path, with a size guard | locked | `BinaryFileData["mimeType"]` is widened to admit the video media types (D-66), and `VIDEO_MIME_TYPES` is added beside `IMAGE_MIME_TYPES` in `packages/common/src/constants.ts`. The bytes travel the **existing** `presenter:files` path - the same on-demand request an image file uses - because a second message type would duplicate a binary path that already works. The transfer stays **chunk-free**, and the **size guard** is what keeps a big file from becoming an accident: a file over 32 MB is **refused with a clear error**, on both sides (the editor does not send it, the presenter does not take it). D-65's rule still stands for a panel's **live content**: that never travels, only its poster does. |
| D-59 | OCR is an in-app capability; the engine is `tesseract.js`, in the **bridge's Node process** | locked | The engine is `tesseract.js` (WASM) and it runs in the **bridge's Node process** - never in the browser: a multi-megabyte download is not what a classroom connection is for, and the project rule is no new dependency without the owner's approval (D-11). The app **works with or without** the bridge: the option is offered either way, and with no bridge the app says plainly that **OCR needs the bridge** instead of failing silently. The contract is one definition, shared: `OcrRequest { id, imageBase64, region?, language? }` and `OcrResult { id, text, lines, confidence? }`. The **region** travels in the request, in the image's own pixels: the ROI crop is the one OCR lever that costs nothing and buys the most (D-55). The engine's own rules: **one worker**, created once and surviving every read; the language data **not** uncached; the core **not** pinned to a single file, so the loader can choose the SIMD core where the CPU has one. |
| D-82 | the live editor view is a **window capture**, and the frames never reach the document | locked | The owner asked for the ACTUAL VS Code window on the canvas, not a reconstruction of its text. D-80 said the panel cannot be a pixel mirror of the window, because the extension API has no screenshot facility - and that is still true of the API. But the API is not the only way out of the editor process. **What was measured on the teacher's machine on 2026-10-22**: `ffmpeg`'s `gdigrab`, asked for a window by its handle (`-i hwnd=0x...`), returns a **BLACK RECTANGLE** for VS Code (PNG 8,125 bytes, YAVG 16.009) and for VSCodium (PNG 8,145 bytes, YAVG 16.0091), while the same command against an ordinary GDI window (Notepad) returns real content (YAVG 173.221) - the earlier "proof" used Notepad, so it never exercised the case that matters. The reason is that VS Code and VSCodium are **Electron** apps: they render through DirectComposition, and gdigrab's window mode BitBlts from the window's own device context, which does not hold the GPU-composited surface. A **screen** capture fails for a second, independent reason: it shows only what is VISIBLE, so the editor behind the browser shows the browser (YAVG 142.675 against 219.462 for the same editor in the foreground). **What does work** is `PrintWindow` - the standard Windows way to ask a window to render ITSELF into a device context. It understands DirectComposition, and it draws the editor even when something is on top of it: YAVG 219.778 in the foreground and YAVG 219.803 while a topmost Chrome window covers it - the editor, not the occluder - at about 12 ms a frame (10 captures of a 1295x687 window: average 12.0 ms, maximum 16.5 ms). **How** it is captured and encoded is **D-87's**: the capture is the window's **client area** (no OS title bar), at `PrintWindow` flags **3** (`PW_CLIENTONLY` and `PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT` together), encoded as a **JPEG** at quality 60 and scaled to **0.75** - about 43 KB a frame on the wire instead of 154 KB. The capture is a **PowerShell helper that SHIPS INSIDE the extension** (`packages/vscode-bridge/extension/media/capture.ps1`): it finds the window, then loops - `PrintWindow` into a bitmap, encode it, write the bytes to stdout each prefixed with their length as four little-endian bytes - with ONE header line first (`HEADER <width> <height>`, the **scaled** size), so the reader learns the frame's size before the frames arrive. The extension spawns that helper **ONCE** and reads the frames off its running stdout; a helper **per frame** is a bug, not a style, because it would spawn a process for every frame of the whole lesson. The **width and height come from the helper's header line** - the frame's own size, never the panel's size on the canvas and never guessed - and the panel's `<img>` takes the aspect ratio from the picture itself, so the app is never told the size at all. Only the **newest** frame is kept: the panel wants the editor as it is now, so frames that arrive together are dropped rather than queued; and an **unchanged** picture is not published again at all (D-88). **The frames never travel over the websocket, and they never reach the element payload.** The payload is saved with the document, so a frame in it would put the teacher's screen into the `.excalidraw` file - and since wave 5i the frames are **not even messages**: the extension serves them as an **MJPEG stream** from the HTTP server it already has, and the panel plays that stream in a plain `<img>` (D-85), authorised by a per-connection capability that dies with the connection (D-86). So the app does **no per-frame work** at all. The library's in-memory registry (`packages/excalidraw/scene/panelFrames.ts`) is still the second channel - never serialized and never inside an element - but what it holds now is the live view's **descriptor**: the capture's state and the stream's URL, not a picture. The **app** pushes that descriptor in when the state changes, and the panel **subscribes** to it; the registry is cleared when the bridge session ends. The **text mirror stays the fallback**: with no window, a helper that cannot start, a helper that died, or a machine that is not Windows, the panel shows the mirrored text and **says why** (D-75) - never a blank region. There is **nothing to install and no dependency to add**: PowerShell is on every Windows machine, `child_process` is a Node builtin, and the extension's manifest still has **no `dependencies` field** (AC-64). The frame rate is a **setting** (`excalidraw-bridge.captureFps`, 6 a second by default), and the helper is found relative to the extension's own installed path. |
| D-83 | the **native editor is the code panel's DEFAULT**, and the live window is opt-in | superseded by D-99 | The owner's words, 2026-10-22: "what happened to native code editor inside the canvas which was the fallback lets make it default". Wave 5g made the live window the panel's **automatic** view - the condition was one expression (`capture?.status === "live" ? <img> : <editor>`), so the picture won whenever the bridge produced one, and the native editor became the fallback. That is inverted: the native editor is what the teacher reads, it is crisp, it is restyleable, and it shows the editor's own text. So the panel's **VIEW** decides. The view is a property of the **panel**: it lives in the code panel's own widening of the payload (`CodePanelContent = PanelContent & { code: CodePanelState or null; view?: CodePanelView }`), NOT in the shared `PanelContent.options` and NOT in `packages/element` - one writer (`withPanelView`) and one reader (`readPanelView`), in the same style as `withCodePanelState` / `readCodePanelState`. `readPanelView` returns `"editor"` for a payload that says nothing, so a panel created before this wave reads as the default. The element update is `panelViewUpdate(content, view)` -> `{ panel }`, in the same style as `panelLockUpdate` / `panelUnlockUpdate` (`packages/excalidraw/scene/lockPanel.ts`), and the write goes through `mutateElement` - the same path the lock button uses (`App.togglePanelLock`). The view **survives the bridge's own writes**: the bridge rewrites the panel's payload whenever the teacher's editor changes - at most every 100 ms, with the last state always published (D-88) - and `withCodePanelState` is `{ ...content, code: state }`, so the view is kept. The teacher flips the view from the panel's OWN header (`code-panel-header`), which is always mounted - NOT from `PanelChrome.tsx`, whose chrome is rendered only for SELECTED panels (`App.tsx` filters on `selectedElementIds`), so a button there would be invisible until the panel was selected, and the teacher must be able to flip the view mid-class without selecting anything. The button is not rendered on a MIRROR: the presenter shows the host's view and does not change it (D-57). The reason line (`code-panel-live-status`) is shown ONLY in the live view: in the default view the teacher did not ask for the live view, so there is nothing to explain (D-75). **SUPERSEDED (wave 5m, D-99):** the owner asked for the opposite on 2026-10-22 ("i need to click live editor (why is it not on by default) to check the output"), so the live window is the DEFAULT again and the mirrored editor is the teacher's own choice. Everything else in this row still holds: the view is the panel's own property, it is persisted in the payload, it survives the bridge's writes, and the presenter never gets the button. |
| D-84 | the editor's grammar comes from a **registry** the library owns and the app fills | locked | The owner asked for "day 1 support for javascript, java, python, c, c++ and rust languages", and the reason it was needed is measured: `CodeMirrorEditor.tsx` applied `mermaidLite()` to EVERY CodeMirror instance, so a Python file was highlighted as if it were a mermaid diagram - `def` and `import` were matched by the hand-written grammar's "node name" rule and came out as VARIABLE names. The mermaid grammar belongs to the text-to-diagram dialog, which is the only thing that speaks mermaid. So the grammar is chosen by the **language**, and the library cannot own the grammars: `@excalidraw/excalidraw` is a published package and five `@codemirror/lang-*` dependencies would add megabytes to every consumer of it. The library therefore exposes a REGISTRY - `registerCodeMirrorLanguage(id, load)`, `loadCodeMirrorLanguage(id)`, `codeMirrorLanguageIds()` - and the **APP** fills it (`excalidraw-app/codeLanguages.ts`), each grammar behind a **dynamic import**, so a grammar is downloaded only when a file of that language is shown. The library registers its OWN grammar - "mermaid" - itself, at module load (`mermaid-lang-lite.ts`), because it has no dependency; the host never registers it. The **one** rule: a language that is not registered resolves to **NO grammar** (plain text). An unknown file must never be highlighted as something it is not - which is exactly the bug being fixed. The editor holds the grammar in its own `Compartment` (it already had one for the theme and one for the error line) and swaps it when the language changes; until the new grammar arrives the previous one stays, so there is no flash of unhighlighted text and never the WRONG grammar. The grammar and the editor must share **one** CodeMirror instance: `@codemirror/language` defines its `language` facet per COPY of itself, so two copies mean the editor cannot see the grammar at all - the app therefore resolves the same `@codemirror/state`, `@codemirror/language` and `@codemirror/view` the library already has, and no cast is used anywhere to paper over it. `c` is registered with the C++ grammar, because `@codemirror/lang-cpp` is the only C-family grammar CodeMirror ships and C is a subset of C++. |
| D-85 | the live view is an **MJPEG stream** served by the extension's own HTTP server, and the frames never travel over the websocket | locked | Wave 5g sent every frame over the websocket as a base64 PNG, and the app paid for it: **154,512 bytes** on the wire for a **115,884-byte** PNG (base64 inflation), a JSON parse, a ~154 KB data URL built in JavaScript, a DOM swap and a layout - **3.3 ms of main thread** and **5 React re-renders of the panel a second**, all to move a picture the browser can decode on its own. The idea worth taking from WindowStream is its **TRANSPORT**: the frames are served as `multipart/x-mixed-replace` and the browser plays them in a plain `<img>`. It was measured on this machine on 2026-10-22: a cross-origin `<img src="http://127.0.0.1:PORT/stream">` pointed at such a server loaded in **0 ms**, with `naturalWidth`/`naturalHeight` = the frame's own 1295x687, and it painted the real editor window. So the extension serves the frames from the HTTP server **it already has** (the same `node:http` server that carries the websocket upgrade, the same fixed loopback port, a different path), and the panel's live view is a plain `<img src={streamUrl}>`. The app then does **nothing** per frame: no data URL, no registry entry per frame, no re-render per frame - it re-renders when the CAPTURE **STATE** changes, not when a picture arrives. The frames therefore do not even reach the app's data: they never travel over the websocket, so they cannot reach the element's payload either, and D-82 gets **stronger** rather than weaker. The boundary is `frame` and each part carries `Content-Type: image/jpeg`, `Content-Length` and `X-Seq`. |
| D-86 | the stream is authorised by a **per-connection capability** in the URL path, and the session token never appears in a URL | locked | The websocket authenticates **in band** (D-50, D-67): the token is in the FIRST frame, never in a URL, because a URL ends up in the browser's history, in a proxy log and in a `Referer` header. The stream cannot use the token in its URL for the same reason, and it does not need to: a URL path segment may hold a **single-purpose, per-connection capability**. When the extension accepts a connection it draws **32 hex characters** from `randomBytes(16)` - unguessable, and **per connection**: two connections get two different capabilities, and a capability stops working the moment its connection closes, because it dies with the connection. The capability travels to the app in the `bridge:hello` the extension already sends, as `streamUrl` (`http://127.0.0.1:<port>/s/<capability>/frame`), and a request that carries an unknown capability gets a **404**. The session token is **never** in that URL: the two secrets have different jobs, different lifetimes and different places. |
| D-87 | the capture is the **client area**, a **JPEG** at quality 60, scaled to **0.75** | locked | `PrintWindow` with `PW_CLIENTONLY` and `PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT` together (**flags 3**) was measured on the teacher's live VS Code window on 2026-10-22: mean luminance **215.899**, the same as flags 2, and it renders the **client area** - **1282x674** for a 1297x681 window, so the OS title bar and the borders are gone and the picture is the editor itself. The bitmap is therefore sized from `GetClientRect` + `ClientToScreen` (the client rectangle on the screen) and not from `GetWindowRect`. The **format** is JPEG, not PNG: GDI+'s JPEG encoder with `Encoder::Quality` at **60**. The **scale** is **0.75**, drawn with a bilinear `DrawImage` before the encoding. The sizes were measured on that same window: PNG 115,884 B (154,512 B base64), JPEG q85 116,166 B, q75 93,736 B, **q60 73,922 B**, q45 63,591 B, **q60 at 0.75 scale 45,091 B**, q60 at 0.5 scale 25,679 B - and the mean luminance stays ~232 at every setting, so the picture is still real content. The helper's `HEADER <width> <height>` line reports the **scaled** output size, and the length prefix in front of each frame stays exactly as it was (four little-endian bytes). The quality and the scale are **parameters** of the helper (`-Quality`, `-Scale`) with those defaults, so they can be changed without editing the script. |
| D-88 | an **unchanged picture is not published again**, and the editor's text is published **at most every 100 ms**, with the last state always published | locked | Two kinds of waste, one rule: **do not redo what has not changed**. The **picture**: the helper's JPEG encoding is deterministic, so an unchanged editor produces byte-identical JPEG; the extension computes `zlib.crc32(jpegBytes)` (a Node builtin - no dependency) and compares it with the CRC of the last published frame, and an unchanged picture is **not written to the stream again**. The change detection lives in the **extension**, not in the helper: the helper would have to read its own pixels back to know, and the extension already holds the bytes. A static editor therefore costs **no traffic and no app work at all**. The **text**: `vscode.workspace.onDidChangeTextDocument` fires on **every keystroke**, and `editorState.ts` puts the **whole file** in every message, so every keystroke made the app rewrite the whole scene. The publish is now **coalesced to at most one every 100 ms**, and the **last** state is **always** published (a trailing timer), so the final text is never lost. The whole-file payload stays - a diff is a bigger change than this wave is for - because the coalescing is what removes the per-keystroke scene rewrite. The 100 ms is a **constant with a comment**, not a number scattered through the code. |
| D-89 | the extension may **declare** its dependencies, and `tesseract.js` is declared and **shipped inside the `.vsix`** | locked | The owner asked for "tesseract.js as a declared extension" (2026-10-22): the OCR engine stops being a run-time install the teacher has to arrange and becomes a **real dependency of the extension**, shipped inside the `.vsix`. This **amends AC-64**, which said the extension's manifest has no dependencies - it is the FIRST dependency the extension declares, and it is declared in the extension's own manifest, not in the library's or the app's (D-59, AC-127). The extension is **not** one of the root yarn workspaces (`packages/*` does not reach `packages/vscode-bridge/extension`), so the **root** `package.json` carries `tesseract.js` as the build-time source the `.vsix` is built from - that is why the root `package.json` and the root `yarn.lock` change, and it is the mechanical consequence of the owner's decision, not a new dependency of the product. The `.vsix` ships the dependency tree **as files** (`tesseract.js`, `tesseract.js-core` with its `.wasm` cores, and the runtime dependencies), because tesseract.js loads its worker and its core from paths at run time - bundling the extension's TypeScript with esbuild is **not enough** - and the build **fails loudly** when a file it needs is missing, because a `.vsix` that installs but cannot read is worse than one that does not install. The **declared copy is loaded first** (the one inside the extension's own installed folder) and the run-time install stays as the **fallback**, so a teacher who put `tesseract.js` in the project they teach from still gets OCR. The engine's own rules do not change: **one worker**, created once; the language data **not** uncached; the core **not** pinned to a single file, so the loader can pick the SIMD core. |
| D-90 | the OCR engine is **chosen by a setting**: `tesseract` in-process, `indra` and `paddle` as Python sidecars | locked | The owner asked for "paddle-ocr and indra ocr ... as options because they are better performance wise" (2026-10-22), so the engine became a **choice**: `excalidraw-bridge.ocrEngine` is `tesseract`, `indra` or `paddle` and defaults to `tesseract`; `excalidraw-bridge.pythonPath` names the Python the Python engines use (default `python`). The Python engines **cannot be npm dependencies** - they are Python - so they run as a **sidecar** the extension spawns **once** and talks to over stdio, one JSON object per line, exactly the shape the capture helper already uses (D-82, AC-120). The sidecar uses `importlib.util.find_spec` to report the engines it can reach **without importing them** (`find_spec` does not execute the package, so asking "is paddleocr there?" must not pay for paddleocr's ten-second import), imports the chosen engine **lazily** on the first request, and keeps the model loaded for every later request - the model load is the expensive part. The sidecar's lines are in the **image's own pixels**, exactly like tesseract's, so the app's placement code does not change (D-59). The engine is resolved **once**, at activation, and the panel is told which engine is in use; `ocr:request` gains an optional `engine` (the app may ask for one, and the extension falls back to its setting when the field is absent) and `ocr:result` gains an optional `engine` and an optional `durationMs` - both optional in the runtime guard, so an extension that predates them still connects. The engine is **never in the browser** (D-59). |
| D-91 | an engine that is **not installed** is an EXPLICIT, actionable state, never a silent failure | locked | A read that fails **silently** is worse than one that fails loudly (D-59, D-75): the teacher would see a panel with no text and no idea why. So the extension's OCR state is `ready` (with the engine's name) or `unavailable` (with a reason), and the reason names the **exact command** that fixes it - `pip install indra-ocr` or `pip install paddleocr paddlepaddle`. For PaddleOCR the reason also says the truth about this machine: **PaddlePaddle ships wheels for cp310, cp311, cp312 and cp313 only** - there is **no cp314 wheel on any version** - so PaddleOCR needs **Python 3.12 or 3.13**, not the 3.14 the owner's default Python is. The sidecar knows its own `sys.version_info`, so it can say it, and it says it about **its own** interpreter rather than about a guess. The app shows the reason, and the benchmark records the same reason for an engine that could not be measured (AC-126): PaddleOCR is a fact to **report** on this machine, not a failure to hide. |
| D-92 | the chrome is sized for the **SCREEN** and the content for the **PANEL**, and the panel's own transform is the ONE source of the scale | locked | A panel is a DOM overlay, and the overlay draws it with a `transform: translate(...) scale(<zoom>) ...`, so everything inside the panel is multiplied by the canvas zoom before it reaches the screen. The **CONTENT** wants that: the code and the picture are the panel's own, and they grow and shrink with it. The **CHROME** does not: the header, the control strip, the buttons, the sliders and the selects are the teacher's instrument panel, and they have to be readable and hittable at **every** zoom. What was measured on the owner's machine on 2026-10-22 at 51% zoom: the code panel's header box was **11 screen pixels** tall (its text overlapped) and the video's control buttons were about **5** - the controls could not be seen and could not be hit. So every chrome size is a **SCREEN** size, and it is put into the panel's own units by dividing it by the scale; the transform then brings it back to the size it was meant to have. The scale has **ONE** source: `panelScale(camera)` is the very number `panelTransform` folds into the panel's transform, and it is published on the panel element as the CSS custom property `--panel-scale`, so the transform and the chrome can never disagree about the zoom - nothing reads the zoom a second time and no number is hard-coded. The chrome's sizes live in **one** place (`packages/excalidraw/scene/panelChrome.ts`) and are applied inline, because a size that cannot be measured cannot be defended (AC-128); the stylesheet keeps the chrome's **arrangement**, and divides its spacing by the same `--panel-scale`. The chrome **outside** the panel - the lock button, the bridge field, the OCR button - is positioned in viewport coordinates and is already unscaled, so it does not change. |
| D-93 | the panel is never blank: a region with nothing in it says **WHY** | locked | D-75 says a failure is a message, not a silence, and the code panel broke it: with the bridge not connected the panel's source region rendered an **empty editor** - a black box with no explanation - which is exactly what the owner saw on 2026-10-22. The rule is that a region which has nothing to show says so, in the panel's own words: the bridge is not connected (the reconnect state), no file is open in the editor, the file is open but **EMPTY**, the capture is unavailable (the live view's own reason), or the video's file is gone from the document. A blank region is never an answer, and a missing file is never an empty frame. The wording lives in the labels (`labels.panel.code.*`, `labels.panel.fileGone`), so the panel and the rest of the app say the same thing, and the reason is never invented in the component. |
| D-94 | a video bigger than **32 MB** does not become a base64 data URL: it is stored as a **Blob in the origin's own IndexedDB** | locked | The base64 data URL is the right shape for a **picture** (a few hundred KB) and the wrong shape for a **video** (hundreds of MB). So the threshold is the same number the presenter's files channel already uses — **32 MB** — and the two are the same decision seen from two sides. A video at or under the threshold keeps the **existing** path untouched (the file store, the `dataURL`, the export, the `.excalidraw` file): nothing about the small case changes, so nothing about the exports changes. A video **over** the threshold is written to a **new store** — `media-db` / `media-store`, the same `idb-keyval` helpers `excalidraw-app/data/LocalData.ts` already uses for `files-db` — as the **`File`/`Blob` itself**. IndexedDB stores a Blob **by reference**, so the bytes go to the browser's own disk and never into the JavaScript heap: a 620 MB video costs the tab nothing. The document then carries a **small id**, not the video. The id is **random** (`nanoid`), not a hash of the bytes: hashing would mean reading the whole file again, which is the very thing this decision exists to avoid. |
| D-95 | the **library** resolves a media-backed panel through a **registry the host fills**; the library never touches IndexedDB | locked | `packages/excalidraw` cannot reach the app's store (the library must not depend on the app, and it gains no dependency). So the library owns the **shape** and the app owns the **bytes**: `packages/excalidraw/scene/mediaRegistry.ts` holds one resolver — `(mediaId) => Promise<string \| null>` — that returns an **object URL** for a media id, and the app registers its own at startup. The panel's source grows one variant, `{ type: "media", mediaId, mimeType }`, beside the existing `{ type: "file", fileId, mimeType }`. The **element's** payload type lives in `@excalidraw/element`, outside this wave's scope, so the widened union is declared in the library itself (`PanelSource`) and `panelSource()` is the only way to read a panel's source back - the widening is paid for in one place, the same rule the code panel follows. The library asks the registry, gets a URL, and renders a plain `<video src={url}>`. No IndexedDB code, no `idb-keyval`, no blob handling in the library. |
| D-96 | the **presenter resolves the media itself**, from the same origin's store, so a big video reaches it with **no transfer and no size limit** | locked | The presenter is a **second window of the same origin** (`http://localhost:3001`), so it has the **same IndexedDB** as the editor. The media does not have to travel at all: the presenter registers the same resolver, reads the same `media-store`, and makes its own object URL. The bytes never cross the `BroadcastChannel`, so there is nothing to be too big. The presenter's existing **32 MB guard stays exactly where it is** — it is the guard of the **legacy file path** (`presenter:files`, the `BinaryFileData` with its `dataURL`), and it keeps doing its job there. The change is that a big video no longer **uses** that path. The panel's element travels to the presenter as it always did — an element with a payload carrying a **short id** — and the presenter fills in the bytes itself. |
| D-97 | a video in a container the browser **cannot play** is never silent: the panel says so and shows the command that fixes it | locked | A panel that shows nothing and says nothing is indistinguishable from a panel that has nothing to show (the same rule as D-91 and D-75). So the `<video>` element's own `error` event is the **oracle**: `MEDIA_ERR_SRC_NOT_SUPPORTED` and `MEDIA_ERR_DECODE` are the browser saying "I cannot play this", and the panel then says exactly that — which format, and the command that converts it: `ffmpeg -i "input.mkv" -c:v libx264 -c:a aac -movflags +faststart "output.mp4"`. The sentence is in the **i18n file**, not in the component. The insert is **not refused** — the teacher is never told "no"; the video is inserted and the panel explains itself, and the sidebar says the same thing **before** the insert so the teacher can decide to convert first. |
| D-98 | the video row in the Files list **drags** onto the canvas, and a **loading wireframe** stands where the player will land | locked | Today the row is `draggable={image}` (`excalidraw-app/sidebar/FilesTab.tsx:609`): an image drags, a video does not, and that is the whole of the owner's complaint. The video takes the **same** path as the image — `armImageDragOnCanvas` becomes a path that can carry a video — and the release point is the drop point, exactly as it is for a picture. The difference is the **wait**: a big video takes a moment to be read and stored, so the canvas shows a **wireframe rectangle** of the player at the drop point while that happens, and the real panel replaces it. The wireframe is drawn by the app's own overlay, in the same style as the image drag's ghost, and it is **removed** when the insert finishes or fails. |
| D-99 | the code panel's **default view is the live window**, and a panel the teacher never switched follows the default | locked | The owner asked for the opposite of D-83 on 2026-10-22 ("i need to click live editor (why is it not on by default) to check the output"), so `DEFAULT_CODE_PANEL_VIEW` becomes `"live"`. The default alone is not enough: wave 5h wrote `view: "editor"` into every panel's payload, and that was the **old default**, not a choice the teacher made. So the panel records whether the view was **chosen**: a panel whose payload carries no choice follows the default, and a panel the teacher switched keeps what the teacher switched to (D-83's promise — the teacher's choice survives a reload — stands, and gets stronger). The choice is a **flag beside** the view, not a second view string, so a payload written before this wave still loads. |
| D-101 | the editor publishes the live view's **descriptor** to the presenter, and the presenter writes it into its own registry | locked | The live view is a **descriptor** — the capture's state and the stream's URL — and it lives in the library's in-memory registry because a picture must never reach the document (D-82, D-85). The presenter is a second context, so it needs the descriptor **handed to it**. The editor publishes it over the **presenter channel** it already has (the same `BroadcastChannel` that carries the scene), and the presenter writes it into **the same registry** the editor uses (`setPanelCapture`, one call per bridge code panel). The URL carries the connection's **capability** (D-86) and the **session token is never in a URL** (D-50) — handing the descriptor to a **same-origin window the teacher opened** does not widen it: a `BroadcastChannel` is readable only by this origin's own pages. The capability still dies with its connection: when the bridge reconnects the editor gets a new URL and publishes it, and the presenter follows. A presenter that has heard nothing yet shows **why** (D-75), never a black rectangle. |
| D-102 | a **screenshot** is a button on the panel's chrome, and it captures **one picture**: the editor's window, or the video's paused frame | locked | The two sources are different and the difference is real. The **video's frame** is in the app: the `<video>` element is there, so the app **pauses** it and draws the current frame onto a canvas - no bridge, no extension, no permission. The **editor's window** is not in the app: the frames are served as an MJPEG stream from the extension's own HTTP server (D-85) and the app never sees them. So the extension's server grows **one more path**: the same capability that authorises the stream also authorises a **single** JPEG - the last frame it already holds (it computes that frame's CRC on every publish anyway, D-88) - and the app fetches it with a plain `GET`. The capture therefore costs one HTTP request, no base64 on the websocket, and no new protocol message. Both sources end at the **same place**: an ordinary **image file**, which is what makes the three choices below uniform. |
| D-103 | the three choices appear **only after** the screenshot is taken, and they are the **only** choices | locked | The owner asked for this exactly: "this option should only come up after we click the screen shot option". So the panel's chrome carries **one** button - the screenshot - and nothing else. The capture opens a small **choice strip** with exactly three entries: **OCR the screenshot**, **place on this page**, **place on a new page**. Nothing else appears; the screenshot's own OCR option does not exist before the capture, and the strip closes when a choice is made or the teacher dismisses it. (The panel's **own** OCR button - "Read the text" on the panel's poster or its live frame - is a **different** affordance from D-59 and stays where it is; the strip's OCR entry reads the _captured_ picture, not the panel.) The strip is drawn by the **library** (it is panel chrome, like the header), it is **scaled like the chrome** (the wave 5k rule: the chrome keeps its size on screen at any zoom), and it uses the **same theme** as every other affordance this project has added - one set of sizes in `packages/excalidraw/scene/panelChrome.ts`, one set of colours, one button shape. |
| D-104 | placing a screenshot shows a **wireframe** where it will land, and the teacher places it | locked | The owner asked for the same affordance the sidebar's images already have: "a wire frame of where to place the image similiar to the draggablity of gif and images from the sidebar". So after a capture, a **wireframe rectangle** - the picture's own size, in the chrome's own style - stands on the canvas where the picture will land, and the teacher moves it and clicks to place it. The wireframe is removed when the picture lands, when the choice is dismissed, and when the panel is deleted. The picture itself is an ordinary **image element**, so everything the teacher can do with an image (crop, delete, move to another page by cut and paste, export) works on a screenshot with no extra code. |
| D-105 | "OCR the screenshot" is the **same** OCR the project already has, and the OCR option is also offered for an ordinary image | locked | The project already reads text out of a picture: the OCR request carries the picture's bytes and the ROI, and the result places per-line text elements over it (D-59, AC-73). A screenshot is a picture, so the screenshot's OCR **is** that path - not a second OCR. The owner also asked "maybe we can add this to regular images aswell i suppose": an ordinary image element on the canvas gets the same **OCR** choice, so the teacher can read the text out of a screenshot they pasted in by hand. The engine is still the extension's (D-90) and never in the browser (D-59); with no bridge the option says so plainly (D-91). |
| D-106 | an engine that **crashes on its first real read** falls back to a configuration that works, and the fall-back is **recorded** | locked | The sidecar's contract is a **line per request** and an answer that is either a result or an error. A crash on the first read therefore has to become **a working answer**, not a dead engine. The rule is narrow and mechanical: the sidecar builds the PaddleOCR model, and if the **first** read raises the oneDNN/PIR error, it rebuilds the model with **`enable_mkldnn=False`** and reads again - **once**, and the model that works is kept for every later read (D-90). The fall-back is **not** unconditional: oneDNN is faster when it works, so the sidecar tries the fast path first and only drops to the slow one when the fast one has actually failed. The fall-back is **reported** in the answer - the engine's name and whether it ran with oneDNN - so the teacher can see why a Paddle read took 28 seconds instead of 300 milliseconds, and the extension's benchmark records the same fact (AC-126). A **second** failure is an error, not a third try: the sidecar does not loop. |
| D-107 | the sidecar's **fall-back** is a property of the engine's model, not of the request | locked | The model is loaded **once** and kept (D-90), so the decision "does this engine need the slow path" belongs to the model, not to the request that happens to arrive first. The sidecar therefore remembers, per engine, **how the model was built**, and every later request reuses that model. A request never rebuilds a model behind the teacher's back, and the model load - which is the expensive part - is paid **once**. The state lives in the sidecar's own module state, next to the models, and it is **reported** in the `ready` line, so the extension can tell the app which engines are ready and how (D-90, D-91). |
| D-108 | the chrome's **icon-only** buttons have a definite width that the flexbox may not shrink | locked | A chrome control that is sized for the **screen** (D-92) must be exactly that size on screen, and an icon-only button is the one case where a flexbox can quietly take that size away: the button's content is an `<svg>` with a `viewBox` and no intrinsic width, and the stylesheet sizes that svg in **percentages** of the button - so the button's width depends on its content and its content depends on its width, and the browser resolves the circle to **zero**. The fix is two rules, both mechanical: the button's **inline** style carries `flex-shrink: 0` (so the flexbox may not shrink a control whose size is already decided), and the stylesheet gives the icon an **absolute** size instead of a percentage (so it never depends on the button's width at all). The rule is the chrome's own: **a control's size is decided by `scene/panelChrome.ts` and nothing may take it away**. It is recorded here because jsdom cannot lay out a flexbox, so the check has to be a **style** check - the button's inline style must carry the rule - and because the defect was invisible to every unit test and only a real browser saw it. |
| D-109 | the benchmark record must say **where** each engine was measured, and a number measured on another machine is still a number | locked | A record that says "paddle is unavailable" reads as a fact about paddle, and it is not one - it is a fact about the machine the record was written on (Python 3.14, no paddle wheel). The record therefore names, for every engine, **where** it was measured and **what** it was measured with, and an engine measured **elsewhere** is recorded with its numbers, its Python and its date, and is marked as such. A missing engine is still a **missing** engine on the machine that runs the benchmark - that stays "unavailable, and here is why" - but it is no longer allowed to stand as the engine's verdict. The benchmark's own test therefore checks that the record is **internally true** (every engine either carries measured numbers with its provenance, or says plainly that it could not be measured **here** and why), and not that a particular engine is absent. |
| D-110 | the extension ships the **engine and the Python path** as its defaults, so installing it is enough | locked | A setting's value lives in the user's settings; a **default** lives with the extension, and VS Code takes the default when the user has not chosen a value. So the two settings that name _this machine's_ tools - the OCR engine and the interpreter the Python engines run in - are the extension's to ship: the manifest declares them with the owner's values, and VS Code applies them until the teacher overrides them. The **token** is the one setting the extension must NOT ship: it is a **secret**, the `.vsix` is a **published** artifact (it lives in a public repository), and the token's whole purpose is to be **per-session** (D-50) - so it stays the teacher's, set once in the settings UI. The rule this records: **a setting that names a tool ships with the extension; a setting that holds a secret never does.** |
| D-111 | the app's token lives for the **tab's session**, so a reload does not ask for it again | locked | The app holds the token in a closure and nowhere else (D-50): never in `localStorage`, never in a URL, never in the document. The cost of that strictness is that **every page reload** asks the teacher to paste the token again - and the teacher reloads the page often (the extension changes, the dev server restarts). The browser already has a memory that is exactly right for this: **`sessionStorage`**, which belongs to the **tab**, survives a reload, and is dropped when the tab closes. It is still not `localStorage` (which would outlive the tab and the session), still not a URL, still not the document - so the secret's blast radius is unchanged, and the paste becomes **once per tab** instead of once per reload. The token is still never written to the document, never put in a URL, and still dies with the tab. |
| D-112 | the capture **re-resolves the editor's window** whenever it stops producing a picture, and the extension **restarts** a helper that has died | locked | The helper resolves the window once and then loops with that handle, so a window that is hidden, minimised or recreated (a Reload Window) leaves it holding a dead handle: `PrintWindow` returns black, or the helper exits with `NO_WINDOW` and the panel says _"The live view has not started yet"_. That is a **fact about a moment**, and it was being answered with a **permanent** failure. So the helper keeps the _rule_ for finding the window (the process, the title) and re-resolves the _handle_ whenever a capture comes back empty, and it does **not** exit when there is no window - it waits and says so. The extension restarts a helper that has exited, and re-arms the capture when a client connects, so a reload recovers **by itself** instead of needing a refocus. The rule: **a capture that cannot find its subject must keep looking, and the subject is found by rule, not remembered by handle.** |
| D-113 | the editor's window is found **by rule**, even when it is not the foreground window | locked | `Get-Process`'s `MainWindowHandle` is **0** for a hidden or minimised window, so the lookup that finds the editor while it is in front **loses it** the moment the browser takes the focus - which is the normal state while the teacher is looking at the whiteboard. So the window is found by **enumerating the editor's own top-level windows and matching the title** (the same title rule the project already uses elsewhere), and a window that exists but is not visible is a **different, stated** fact from a window that does not exist. The panel then says _"VS Code's window is not visible - click on it"_, not a black rectangle and not "not started yet". The rule: **"not in front" and "not there" are different facts, and neither of them is "broken".** |
| D-114 | every part of the chrome is sized by the **one** source of the scale, including the choice strip and the wireframe | locked | Wave 5k made the chrome's **sizes** screen-sized and put the one conversion in `scene/panelChrome.ts`. But the sizes only reach the DOM through the **inline styles**, and the parts added later - the screenshot's choice strip, the wireframe - were written without asking the chrome first: the strip's buttons got a height and a font size and **no width**, and the strip's `top` came from the caller. So a control that exists in the DOM is not a control the teacher can see or hit. So the rule is now stated for the whole chrome, not for the header: **nothing the chrome draws may size itself**; every size comes from `panelChrome.ts`, and a control that cannot be sized by the chrome must not be drawn. The strip's three buttons are laid out in a row with a real width each, and the strip hangs **on the panel** - its position is computed from the panel's own rect, never guessed. The test is a **measurement**, not an assertion about a style: a control that is **0 px wide** is a control that does not exist (the 0 px screenshot button of wave 5p was exactly this). |
| D-115 | the screenshot button belongs to the **panel**, not to the selection, and **every** panel kind has one | superseded by D-135 | The camera is a property of a panel (it captures that panel), so it must be reachable **whenever the panel is on screen** - not only after the teacher selects it. And a **video** panel can be captured (the owner's item 5.4: the video has no camera at all) - so the camera is on the video's chrome as well as the code's. The rule: **a control that acts on a panel lives on the panel**, visible and hit-testable without any prior selection. |
| D-116 | taking a screenshot **brings the screenshot forward**: the canvas darkens, the preview is the thing in focus, and the three choices belong to it | locked | The owner asked for the visual effect of a **z-axis**: "after clicking this button the preview of the screenshot should appear as if it has moved up in the z axis and the canvas/editor has moved back in the z - axis (visual effect only darkens background, leaving the screenshot to become the thing in focus with those 3 options i mentioned, also add a cropping tool that is turned on by default so we can crop the screenshot and revert the crop back one step aswell)". So the capture does not merely produce a picture: it opens a **focus state** - the canvas behind is dimmed, the captured picture is presented in front, the three choices act on **that** picture, and the picture can be **cropped** before it is placed (the crop is on by default, and one step takes it back). The crop is part of the picture's **own** state - it belongs to the screenshot, not to the canvas - and "revert one step" means the crop can be undone exactly once, to the uncropped capture. |
