### Wave 6i — the installed but detached virtual display, and the editor's window

The owner installed the **Virtual Display Driver** (release 25.7.23, "Generic Monitor (VDD by MTT)") on this machine, and asked for the VS Code window to move to it. The driver is installed and its monitor is **detached** - VDD Control keeps a monitor detached until the teacher sets its mode to **Extend** - so the extension had nothing to report and said "no virtual display" (D-146). The teacher then set it to Extend, and measured a second truth on their own window: `SetWindowPos` on a **maximised** window returns `true` and the window does not move, so a move that did not happen must never be reported as one (D-150), and a window that was moved must be able to come **back** (D-149). The rule: **an installed but detached virtual display is a fact, and the fact names the click** (D-146); the **editor's own window** moves only when the teacher asks for it (D-148), **by rule** and without taking the focus (D-147); and the move is **measured**, not believed (D-150). The acceptance row for this wave is **AC-183**.

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-183 | an **installed but detached** virtual display is reported with the click that attaches it, the **editor's window** is moved onto the virtual display **by rule** and only when the teacher asks, and a window that was moved can be **brought back** | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/virtualDisplay.test.ts` | The teacher may have a virtual display driver **installed** and its monitor **detached**, and that is a fact the extension reports instead of hiding (D-146): the helper no longer skips an adapter with no current mode, the adapter is reported as a virtual display that is **detached**, and the reason names the click - open **VDD Control** and set the monitor to **Extend**. A detached display is never chosen as the target, because there is nothing to place a window on, and the input panel shows the remedy instead. The editor's own window is found **by rule** - the workspace name in the window's title, the same rule the capture uses (D-112, D-113) - and the placement moves it without taking the focus (SWP_NOACTIVATE), so the teacher keeps typing while their window is on the projector (D-147). It moves **only when the teacher presses the panel's button** (D-148), and it comes **back** - to the primary display, in the size it had, maximised as it was if it was maximised (D-149). The move is **measured**: a maximised window is un-maximised first without taking the focus, the rect is read back, and a window that did not land where it was asked to is said so plainly (D-150). Tests: `AC-183 an installed but detached virtual display is reported with the click that attaches it`, `AC-183 the editor's window is moved onto the virtual display without taking the focus`, `AC-183 a window that was moved can be brought back, and a move that did not happen is reported as one`. |

### Wave 6j — what the live clips found

The feature clips for the public release were recorded by **driving the real app with a real editor behind it**. Doing that surfaced five defects the tests did not, and two of them mean a **shipped install does not work at all**: the `.vsix` does not carry the virtual-display helper, so every install has a **dead virtual display**; and the snapshot endpoint sends no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, so a **stock** browser refuses the app's `fetch()` of the editor's picture - which breaks "take a screenshot of the editor's window" and "crop and read" from the editor. The other three are smaller but real: the mirror picker's choice buttons are painted under the app's bottom page bar, the YouTube caption note is painted under the composited iframe and its background is a theme variable that does not exist, and the input panel's status line writes to `window.status` and so shows nothing. The rule: **the extension ships every helper it runs, and the bridge answers the app's own origin** (D-151).

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-151 | the extension ships **every helper it runs**, and the bridge answers the app's **own** origin | locked | A helper that is not in the `.vsix` is a feature that is **dead in every install**: the extension resolves `media/virtual-display.ps1` inside its own installed folder, and the folder has no such file - so the panel says "no virtual display was found on this machine ... and no display was found at all" on a machine whose Virtual Display Driver is attached as `DISPLAY7`. And the extension's HTTP server must answer the **same loopback origins** its websocket answers: the app is on `http://localhost:3001` and the server is on `http://127.0.0.1:47511`, so the picture the app is **entitled** to fetch was refused by the browser as a cross-origin read - and the only way to record the crop-and-read clip was Chromium's `--disable-web-security` flag, which is how the defect was found. The rule: the **capability stays the authorisation** (D-86) and the server still binds the **loopback interface only**, but a request that carries a live capability and comes from a **loopback** origin is answered with its own origin echoed back, and a request from a **foreign** origin is answered with **no** such header. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-184 | the built `.vsix` **contains** `media/virtual-display.ps1` - and the test reads the `.vsix`'s **entry list**, not its bytes | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/vsix.test.ts` | The `.vsix` is a zip, and the file it carries is read from the zip's **central directory** - the same CRC-checking reader the other tests use. A byte search is not a proof here: the helper's **name** appears inside `extension.js` (the extension resolves the helper by that relative path), so a byte search gives a **false positive**. The entry list is what the installer unpacks, so the entry list is what is checked. Tests: `AC-184 the .vsix ships the virtual-display helper`. |
| AC-185 | the snapshot's response carries an `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for a **loopback** origin, and **omits** it for a **foreign** one | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/stream.test.ts` | The snapshot is the picture the app fetches with a plain `GET` (`fetch()`), so the browser applies the cross-origin rules to it - while the live view is an `<img>`, which is fetched in "no-cors" mode and is not affected. The **same** rule that decides the websocket's origin decides this header: `isAllowedOrigin(origin, extra)`, so a loopback origin (`http://localhost:3001`) gets **its own origin echoed back** and a foreign one (`https://evil.example.com`) gets **no** header at all. The answer also says that the picture's **own size** may be read (`Access-Control-Expose-Headers`): a header that is not CORS-safelisted is hidden from the page's JavaScript, so without it the app would read a size of zero for a picture it can see (D-102). The capability is still the authorisation (D-86): a request without a live capability is still a 404, for every origin. Tests: `AC-185 the snapshot answers a loopback origin with its own origin echoed back`, `AC-185 a foreign origin is answered with no Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. |

### Wave 7k — the setup checks, and installs what is missing

The people this project is for will **not read a README**: a teacher or a student with a laptop, VS Code or VSCodium, and a class to teach. Today they would have to know, on their own, that the project needs Node, that Yarn comes from Corepack, that the extension is installed from a `.vsix`, and that the app's dependencies have to be installed before the app will start. So the project ships a **setup**: one command that **checks what is missing and installs it**. The rule: **the setup reports what it finds, asks before it touches the machine, and never installs software silently** (D-152).

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-152 | the project ships a **setup** that checks for everything it needs and **installs what is missing** | locked | The person who runs the setup is a **teacher or a student**, not a developer, so the setup **reports what it finds** - every thing it checks, in plain words, with the version it found and where it found it - **asks before it installs anything on the machine**, and ends with a **summary** the reader can act on. It never installs software silently: the default is a **question**, `-Yes` accepts the answers, and `-DryRun` reports and installs **nothing** at all. It needs **no dependency of its own** - PowerShell on Windows, bash on macOS and Linux, and nothing else - so it runs from a fresh clone, and **checking never needs the network** (only installing does). It is **safe to run twice**: every check is idempotent, and a second run says _already there_ rather than doing it again. What it checks: Node.js on the PATH **and 20 or newer**, Yarn on the PATH or Corepack to provide it, VS Code (the `code` CLI or the standard install path), VSCodium (the `codium` CLI or the standard install path), the extension's `.vsix` (**built** when it is not there), the app's dependencies (`node_modules` at the repository root), and the **language runtimes** the panel's **Run** can execute (`node`, `python`, `java`). What it installs: Corepack for Yarn, the `.vsix` into whichever editor it found, the app's dependencies, and a missing runtime through **winget** when winget is there - each of them **after asking**. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-186 | the setup **reports** every thing it checks, **asks** before installing, installs **nothing** in `-DryRun`, and the report ends with a summary - and it needs **no dependency of its own** | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/setup.test.ts` | The **checking logic** is a module the test can call - `installer/lib/check.mjs` - and it is a **pure function of what was found**: it takes the versions and the paths the machine has and returns the **report** (what is present, what is missing, what would be installed) and the **plan** (what to ask, what to install, and what a dry run installs - nothing). It **never installs anything**, so the test can feed it a made-up machine and assert on the report: the test needs neither Windows nor a network. The two scripts - `installer/setup.ps1` and `installer/setup.sh` - are the halves that **find** the facts, **ask** and **install**, and the test pins them to the module: every check the module knows about is a check both scripts make, and no script can install anything before its dry-run guard. Tests: `AC-186 the report names every thing the setup checks`, `AC-186 a missing thing is reported as missing with what would be installed`, `AC-186 the setup asks before it installs anything`, `AC-186 a dry run installs nothing`, `AC-186 the report ends with a summary`, `AC-186 the setup needs no dependency of its own`, `AC-186 both scripts check the same things the module checks`, `AC-186 the Windows script's dry run installs nothing`. |

### Wave 7l — the setup as one file, the container, and the guide

The setup of wave 7k **reports** what is missing and installs it - but only where a **package manager** exists, and only for the things that are already on the machine. Recording the end-to-end test in a **Windows Sandbox** - a bare Windows with nothing on it, not even winget - showed what that costs: on such a machine the setup could only _report_, and it had no way to install the **editors** at all. The promise is that the setup installs what is missing, so it must install **everywhere**, not only where a package manager happens to be. And it must be **one file** a teacher can double-click, and the app must also be available as a **container** for a machine on which nothing can be installed at all.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-153 | the setup ships **as one file**, it installs **without a package manager**, and the app also ships as a **container** | locked | Three things follow from the promise. **One file**: the setup is also an **executable** (setup.exe), so it can be double-clicked; it is compiled by the **C# compiler that ships with Windows**, so the project gains **no dependency**, and it carries the installer scripts **inside** it. **Without a package manager**: where winget or brew is there it is used, and where neither is there the setup **downloads the official installer itself** - Node.js from nodejs.org, VS Code and VSCodium from their own sites, each for **the machine's own architecture** (x64 or rm64) - so a bare machine is set up as well as an ordinary one. The **editors themselves** are now something the setup installs, because a teacher with neither VS Code nor VSCodium cannot use the bridge. **A container**: the app is published as an image, so a machine on which nothing may be installed can still serve it; the extension stays on the **host**, because it lives inside the editor, and the container reaches the host's bridge over host.docker.internal. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-187 | the setup **is** one executable, it installs on a machine with **no package manager**, and the container's **build context carries the installer** and has the **heap** the build needs | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/setup.test.ts` | The test reads the **files**: the launcher's source and the build script beside it, the `.dockerignore` and the `Dockerfile`. It asserts that the setup is built by the compiler that ships with Windows - so the project gains no dependency - that the launcher carries the installer scripts with it, that the build context **includes** `installer/` (without it the test's own import cannot resolve inside the build), and that the build is given the **heap** it needs (the build inside a container dies at Node's default 2 GB while the same build on a developer's machine succeeds). The **rules** are pinned too: on a machine with no package manager the setup still installs Node and the editors, because it can download the official installers itself. Tests: `AC-187 the setup is one executable built by the compiler that ships with Windows`, `AC-187 the launcher carries the installer with it`, `AC-187 the container's build context carries the installer and the heap the build needs`, `AC-187 a machine with no package manager is still set up`. |

### Wave 7m — the offline setup: one file, no internet, nothing to install

The setup of wave 7l **downloads** what it installs. A classroom machine may have no internet at all: a locked-down lab, a school network, a laptop on a train. So the project must ship a setup that **carries everything it needs with it** and installs it **offline** - and must also be able to run the app with **nothing installed at all**.

The inventory that decides the payload was taken from the code, not guessed. **The app's npm dependencies are NOT needed at run time**: the app is a **static bundle** served as HTML and assets, so the payload does not carry the npm registry's tarballs and the offline setup never runs `yarn install`. **Python is NOT needed either**: the default OCR engine, _tesseract_, is **WebAssembly inside the extension** and runs in the extension's own process; Python is only for the optional `indra` and `paddle` engines. What the payload therefore carries is the **prebuilt app**, the **`.vsix`**, and the **installers** for Node, VS Code, VSCodium, Python, Java and the virtual display driver.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-154 | the project ships a setup that **needs no internet** and needs **nothing installed** to run the app | locked | Three things follow from the promise. **It carries the payload**: the payload is carried **in pieces** - resources of the executable, `payload.NNNN`, a few megabytes each, with an index (`payload.index.json`) saying which pieces make up which file - and the pieces are written **straight** into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\ExcalidrawClassroom\payload` on the first run, one piece at a time, with each file's **SHA-256 checked as it is written**. A payload that does not match is **refused**, not installed. (Pieces, and not one big resource, because the payload is about 780 MB: a single resource that size has to be held in memory by the runtime and the disk would have to hold it twice - once as the resource, once unpacked - which a small machine cannot do. In pieces, the disk holds the payload once and the memory holds one piece.) **It never reaches the internet**: the internet is needed only when the payload is **built**, by `installer/offline/build-payload.mjs`, which is re-runnable (a file that is already there is skipped) and writes the manifest with each file's hash and version. A file that is missing from the payload is **reported**, never downloaded. The installs are the ones the brief names - Node through `msiexec`, the editors through their own installers, Python through its own, Java from a Temurin zip unpacked into the machine's PATH, the virtual display driver through **its own** installer, and the extension through the editor's `--install-extension` - each **asked for one at a time**, with `-Yes` to accept and `-DryRun` to report and install **nothing**; the parts that need it (the driver, the machine-wide PATH) **elevate the program** with the `runas` verb. The driver needs **administrator rights** and a **reboot**, and the program says both plainly and does not claim the display works before the reboot. **It runs the app with nothing installed**: a built-in `HttpListener` serves the prebuilt app out of the payload and the browser is opened on it - no Node, no Python, no editor, just the whiteboard. And because the app's bridge URL is **baked in at build time** (`VITE_APP_BRIDGE_URL`), the program **prints the URL the app was built with** and says so plainly when it does not match the bridge the user will actually run - it does not pretend. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-188 | the offline setup **carries its payload**, **checks the payload's hashes**, **installs nothing in `-DryRun`**, **runs the app with nothing installed**, and the payload builder **needs no internet at the time it runs** - only at the time it is BUILT | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/offline.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the C# source, the payload builder and the manifest - and asserts on them. It **downloads nothing** and does **not run the payload**: the test proves the SHAPE, and the real run - a Windows Sandbox with **no networking** - proves the thing. The shape it proves is: the C# is **C# 5** (the compiler that ships with Windows understands nothing newer), it carries the payload **in pieces** and writes them out one at a time, it **verifies the SHA-256** of each file as it writes it and **refuses** a payload that does not match, it has **no network code at all**, every install is behind the **`-DryRun` guard**, it **serves the app** with an `HttpListener`, it **prints the bridge URL**, and it **elevates** with the `runas` verb. The payload builder is pinned too: the **URLs** it uses, the **architecture** it takes, that it is **re-runnable**, that it writes the **hash and the version** of each file, and that it never runs `yarn install` - the payload carries no npm tarballs, because the app is a static bundle. The manifest's hash is checked against **node's own SHA-256** of the file, so a manifest that hashes the wrong thing fails. Tests: `AC-188 the offline setup carries its payload with it`, `AC-188 the offline setup checks the payload's hashes and refuses a payload that does not match`, `AC-188 a dry run installs nothing at all`, `AC-188 the offline setup runs the app with nothing installed`, `AC-188 the offline setup never reaches the internet`, `AC-188 the payload builder downloads what the setup needs and is re-runnable`, `AC-188 the manifest carries the hash and the version of every file`, `AC-188 the offline setup is C# 5 and needs no dependency of its own`. |

### Wave 7n — how to confirm it works

The setup of wave 7m installs the app and serves it, but a teacher who installs it on **another** machine is left with two questions the project cannot answer yet. **Is the install sound?** The setup's own report is a by-product of the install: it is gone once the install is over, and it is not a thing a teacher can run afterwards. **Is the behaviour the expected behaviour?** The project has no written description of what each feature should look like, so a deviation stays invisible until a lesson goes wrong - and then nobody can say what the right thing was. Both answers must be readable by a **teacher**, not by a developer, and both live in the **public** repository, so they carry no trace of how the project is built.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-155 | the project ships **a check the teacher runs after the install** and **a checklist of what each feature should look like** | locked | Two documents, both written for a **reader**. **The check** is `installer/verify-install.ps1`: one command, no dependency of its own, and it **only reads** - it installs nothing, changes nothing and removes nothing. It prints a table of one row per check - the check, the expected value, the actual value, and the verdict - and the verdict is one of **PASS** (it is there and it is right), **MISSING** (it is not installed, which is often fine) or **FAIL** (it is there but it is **wrong**, which is the one that needs attention). A missing thing is a row in the table and never an exception, so the script survives a machine where half of it is absent, and it exits 1 only when something FAILED. It ends with a **paste-able report** - the machine's facts, the table and the counts - because that block is how a deviation gets reported. **The checklist** is `docs/VERIFY.md`: for each feature, what to do, what you should see, what a deviation looks like, and the clip that shows it working. The expected values are read from the **code**, never from memory: the Node floor from `installer/lib/check.mjs`, the editor floor from the extension's own `engines.vscode`, the port from the bridge's own `DEFAULT_BRIDGE_PORT`, the extension's name from its own `package.json`, and the hashes from the payload's own `manifest.json`. **The report page** is `docs/REPORT.md`: what you did, what you expected, what you actually saw, which feature, the check report and the machine - and it never asks for the session token, because the token is a secret. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-189 | the install check **reports every thing it checks**, **never changes the machine**, says **MISSING** instead of failing when a thing is absent, and the checklist gives **what to do**, **what you should see** and **what a deviation looks like** for every feature the project has | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/verify.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the script, the checklist and the report page - and asserts on them; it does not run the script, so it needs neither Windows nor the machine's editor. It pins the **rules**: the script installs nothing and changes nothing, every check is guarded so that a missing thing is a row and not an exception, the three verdicts are the ones the legend explains, the script exits 1 only when a check FAILED, the expected values are read from the code rather than written into the script, the report at the end is paste-able, and the checklist gives every feature all four parts and points at a clip. Tests: `AC-189 the check script only reads the machine`, `AC-189 a missing thing is a row and never an exception`, `AC-189 the script ends with a paste-able report and exits 1 only on a failure`, `AC-189 the expected values are read from the code`, `AC-189 the checklist gives every feature its four parts and its clip`, `AC-189 the report page never asks for the session token`. |

### Wave 7o - the offline proof starts from a fresh sandbox

The proof that the offline setup works on a machine with no internet (wave 7m) was **reached**, but not **repeatably**, and the record did not say the whole truth. The `.wsb` asks for no networking, and on the machine it was proved on that request was **not honoured**: the Sandbox came up with a `Microsoft Hyper-V Network Adapter`, a default route, and a working internet. The first explanation was a **stale** sandbox - Windows Sandbox reuses a running virtual machine, and a live session keeps the configuration of the session it was started with. So a **fresh** sandbox was started, with nothing else running, and the adapter was **still** there, the route was **still** there, and the internet **still** answered. The setting is simply not honoured on that machine, and the proof cannot lean on it.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-156 | the offline proof starts from a **fresh** sandbox and says **which of the two** took the internet away | locked | A proof nobody can repeat is not a proof, and a proof that hides how it was reached cannot be trusted either. **Fresh**: Windows Sandbox **reuses a running virtual machine**, and a live session keeps the configuration of the session it was started with - so the proof is started by `installer/offline/run-offline-proof.ps1`, which **stops any Windows Sandbox that is running**, waits for it to be **gone**, writes the `.wsb` for the folder the payload is in (the shipped `.wsb` names one machine's path), starts it, waits for the result and **prints** it. **Honest**: the run **records the network as the Sandbox set it up, before it touches anything** - the adapters, the default route, the proxy - and then says, in words, **which of the two took the internet away**: the `.wsb` (no default route, the internet did not answer) or the run itself. The `.wsb` is kept, because it is the **right** setting, but nothing rests on it: the run takes the network down from inside by **three separate means** - an outbound firewall rule, the **default route removed**, and the **adapter disabled** - and reports **how many of them worked**, because one of them failed on the machine this was proved on. Only then is the machine **probed three ways** - a DNS lookup, a raw TCP connection to a public address, and an HTTP request - and if **any** of them answers, the run says the proof is **not valid** and the setup is not run at all. The proof only ever claims what it measured. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-190 | the offline proof starts from a **fresh** sandbox, takes the network down by **more than one way**, and says **which of the two** took the internet away | `packages/vscode-bridge/tests/offlineProof.test.ts` | The test reads the **files** - the `.wsb`, the run that happens inside it and the launcher that starts it - and asserts on them; it starts no sandbox and touches no network. It pins: the `.wsb` asks for **no networking**, maps the payload folder and runs the proof, and says in its own words that the setting is **not trusted**; the run records the adapters and the default route **before** the takeover, and asks the internet **before** it changes anything; the run **names** the two and says which one it was, deciding it by **facts** (the default route that was there, and whether the internet answered) rather than by an assumption; the takeover uses **three** means and reports **how many** worked; the machine is probed **three ways** and any answer makes the proof **invalid**; and the launcher **stops a running Windows Sandbox**, waits for it to be gone, writes the `.wsb` for **its own** folder, starts it, and prints the result with the verdict taken from the machine's own words. Tests: `AC-190 the sandbox file asks for no networking and runs the proof`, `AC-190 the run records the network as the sandbox set it up, BEFORE it takes it down`, `AC-190 the run says which of the two took the internet away`, `AC-190 the run takes the network down by more than one way, and says how many worked`, `AC-190 the proof is only valid when the machine is really offline`, `AC-190 the launcher starts a sandbox that is fresh, and prints the result`. |

### Wave 7p - the emoji and GIF bar

The owner asked for a bar like the pages bar that holds the emoji and GIFs people actually use, to **drag onto the canvas**, with a **search box** like the phone keyboard's - and **everything in the repo, nothing fetched from a third party**. Popular meme GIFs from Giphy or Tenor are somebody else's copyright and cannot be redistributed, so the assets are **our own**.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-157 | the stickers are **our own artwork**, committed as files, and nothing is fetched from a third party | locked | The emoji are **original simplified vector artwork** drawn from geometric primitives by `excalidraw-app/emoji/tools/build-stickers.mjs`, and the GIFs are **animated renderings of the same artwork** by the same tool, which carries its own GIF encoder - **no dependency is added**. The files are committed under `excalidraw-app/emoji/assets/`, so the bar works with no internet and no API key. They are dedicated to the public domain (CC0) and `docs/STICKERS.md` records the **provenance** of every file: what drew it, the tool, the date, the licence. A bundled set that names a third-party service, key or remote URL in any asset or in the code that loads it is a defect. The size is bounded - one file at most 60 KB and the whole set at most 1.5 MB - so the repo does not bloat. |
| D-158 | one **catalog**, one **pure search** | locked | Every sticker is one row in `excalidraw-app/emoji/catalog.ts`: `id`, `kind` (`emoji` or `gif`), `label`, `tags`, `file`, and its place in the **popularity order** (the order the bar shows with an empty search box). The search is a **pure function** in `excalidraw-app/emoji/search.ts` - it takes the catalog, a query and a kind filter and returns the matching rows - so it is tested without a DOM. The query is trimmed and lower-cased and split into words, **every** word must match the label or a tag, a **prefix** of a label or tag outranks a match in the middle of one, and ties keep the popularity order. An empty query returns the whole set in popularity order, and a query that matches nothing returns an empty list - never the whole set. |
| D-159 | the bar is an **island above the page bar**, with an **Emoji** tab, a **GIF** tab and a **search box** | locked | The bar uses the page bar's visual language (`--island-bg-color`, `--shadow-island`) and sits **above** it, centred in the space the sidebar leaves (D-121). It has two tabs, one search box that searches the open tab, and a scrolling row of stickers. It can be tucked away to a small handle, and **remembers** that (`localStorage`, wrapped in try/catch like the page bar's). A tab with no match says so in words. The search box is a real `input type=search` with a label, and every sticker is a button with an accessible name. |
| D-160 | a sticker lands on the canvas by **drag** or by **click**, on the **page the teacher is on**, and a GIF stays a GIF | locked | **Drag**: the sticker is draggable and carries its id under its own type (`application/x-excalidraw-sticker`); the canvas accepts the drop, **stops the library's own drop handling** for it, and places the sticker by its **centre** at the pointer, converted with the same scene maths the rest of the app uses. **Click**: the sticker lands in the middle of what is visible, clear of the sidebar. Either way it goes through `insertImageIntoScene`, so it is **stamped to the current page** (D-12) and a GIF keeps the `image/gif` type that the animated-image overlay plays. Its long edge is at most the app's image maximum. A drop that carries any other data is left to the library. |

| Row | The acceptance | Check | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC-191 | the bundled set is **complete, valid, small and offline** | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerAssets.test.ts` | The test reads the **files**. Every catalog row has a file that exists; ids are unique; there are at least 40 emoji and at least 12 GIFs; every GIF is a real GIF89a with **more than one frame** and a loop; every emoji is well-formed SVG with a `viewBox`; **no asset or catalog row contains a remote URL** (the SVG namespace aside); the files are within the size bounds; and `docs/STICKERS.md` names every file. Tests: `AC-191 every catalog row has its file`, `AC-191 every gif is animated`, `AC-191 every emoji is a well-formed svg`, `AC-191 nothing names a remote url`, `AC-191 the set is within its size bounds`, `AC-191 the provenance names every file`. |
| AC-192 | the search finds what the teacher means, in the order they would expect | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerSearch.test.ts` | Pure-function tests over the real catalog: an empty query returns the whole set in popularity order, a tag finds its sticker, every word must match, a prefix outranks a middle match, the kind filter holds, case and padding do not matter, and a query that matches nothing returns nothing. Tests: `AC-192 an empty query is the popularity order`, `AC-192 a tag finds its sticker`, `AC-192 every word must match`, `AC-192 a prefix outranks a middle match`, `AC-192 the kind filter holds`, `AC-192 case and padding do not matter`, `AC-192 nothing matches nothing`. |
| AC-193 | the bar shows the two tabs, searches the open tab, and tucks away | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerBar.test.tsx` | Rendered with the editor: the Emoji tab is open first, the GIF tab shows only GIFs, typing narrows the row, a query with no match says so, and the hide control leaves a handle and is remembered across a remount. Tests: `AC-193 the emoji tab is open first and the gif tab shows only gifs`, `AC-193 the search narrows the open tab`, `AC-193 a search with no match says so`, `AC-193 the bar tucks away and remembers it`. |
| AC-194 | a click or a drop puts the sticker on the **current page**, a GIF stays a GIF, and nothing leaves the machine | `excalidraw-app/tests/stickerBar.test.tsx` | With the second page active: a click adds one image element **stamped to that page**; a GIF's file has the `image/gif` type; a drop lands the sticker's **centre** at the pointer, checked **through the library's own conversion** and not a re-derived formula; the library's own drop handling does not also run; a drop of other data is not claimed; and every request the bar makes is **same-origin**. Tests: `AC-194 a click lands on the current page`, `AC-194 a gif keeps its type`, `AC-194 a drop centres the sticker at the pointer`, `AC-194 a drop of other data is left to the library`, `AC-194 nothing leaves the machine`. |

### Wave 7q - the flow studio

The owner asked for a **fully featured creation tool** for flowcharts and tier charts, with **presets** that make an **animated flowchart, an interactive step-by-step process diagram, an animated integration, and node-and-edge flows** quick to make. Wave 6c already gave a selected flowchart a **focus overlay, a loop and an auto-layout** (D-42 to D-44, D-60 to D-63); this wave adds the part that is missing - **making** the chart - and the **animation styles** the owner pointed at. The references and what each one is (read on 2026-10-27): the **Animated Beam** (MagicUI) is a line of light travelling along a curved path between two things, with a curvature, a direction and a duration; **React Flow's animating edges** move a dot along an edge's path, or move a whole node along it; **JointJS's animated process flow** lights up the steps downstream of the one you click. The two CodePens were first refused (**403**) and were then **saved by the owner and read** on 2026-10-27: `Hekutoru/GYeEVV` is **"Process Flow GoJS"** - a process diagram whose pipes are three stacked strokes with a **dashed line that marches** along them - and `sdras/VjvGJM` is **"Animated Flow Chart to demo animating viewBox"** - a click-through decision tree where choosing **Yes** or **No** makes the **camera fly** to the next step and an end step is **drawn on** and then filled. D-168 records what is taken from each and what is not.

| Row | The decision | Status | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D-161 | the studio is a **Flow tab in the sidebar**, and a chart is made of **ordinary elements** | locked | The presets live in a new **Flow** tab beside Files, in the app's sidebar, because that is where the teacher already goes to put things on the page. A chart is **ordinary shapes and arrows** - rectangles, diamonds and ellipses with their **text bound inside**, and arrows **bound at both ends** - exactly as D-42 says of the flowchart it animates: **no new element type**, so a chart can be edited, moved, exported, saved and undone like anything else, and the animation (D-166) plays over it without writing into it. A chart lands on the **page the teacher is on** (D-12), in the middle of what is visible, through the same `appendElements` the sticker bar and the sidebar use. |
| D-162 | a preset is a **pure builder**, in the library, with no DOM | locked | `packages/excalidraw/scene/flowPresets.ts` exports `buildFlowPreset(presetId, labels, options)`, which returns **elements** and touches no DOM, no `appState` and no network - so it is tested without a canvas, and the MCP server (a later wave) calls the **same** function the sidebar does. It lives in `packages/excalidraw` because that is the only package that may import dagre (D-62). Every shape it returns has its **bound text** and its `boundElements` naming the arrows; every arrow's `startBinding` and `endBinding` name **shapes that are in the result**, and the shapes' `boundElements` name the arrow back - both sides, or the chart comes apart the first time a shape is moved. Ids are **unique** and **seeded** by the caller, so the same input gives the same chart. |
| D-163 | the presets | locked | Seven: **process** (a straight run of steps), **decision** (a question with a yes branch and a no branch that rejoin), **steps** (a numbered step-by-step, each step carrying its number), **cycle** (a loop of steps that returns to the first - D-62 already says a loop is expected), **tiers** (stacked layers, each with its own items, for a tier chart or a layered architecture), **integration** (one hub with the things it connects to around it, for "our app talks to these services"), and **network** (nodes joined by edges, for a general node-and-edge flow). Each preset has a **name**, a one-line **description** and a set of **example labels**, so choosing one shows a chart that is already sensible and the teacher only changes the words. |
| D-164 | the chart is filled from a **script**: one line per node | locked | The studio's box takes **text, one line per node** - what a teacher already writes when planning a class. The rules are small and are the same for every preset: a blank line is ignored; a line is a **label**; in **tiers** a line is one tier and the items in it are separated by a vertical bar; in **integration** the **first** line is the hub and each later line is a spoke; in **decision** a line that starts with `?` is a question and the **next two** lines are its yes and no branches; in **network** a line of the form `A -> B` is an **edge** between two nodes that are created the first time their label is seen. A label is trimmed, a label that is empty after trimming is dropped, and a script with **no usable line** builds nothing and says so - it never builds an empty chart. |
| D-165 | the chart **fits the page** | locked | A graph preset (process, decision, steps, network) is laid out with the existing **`layoutFlow`** (dagre, D-62), top-to-bottom or left-to-right, **whichever fits the page better**; a **cycle** is laid out as a **ring**, because a loop reads as a loop only when it goes round, and a line with one long arrow back says something else; **tiers** and **integration** (the hub in the middle, the rest round it) are laid out by plain geometry. Whatever the number of nodes, the chart is **scaled to fit the page's rect** - never larger than the page and never off it - down to a **floor** on the text size (so it stays readable from the back of a room). A script with more nodes than fit at that floor is **reported to the teacher** and not silently shrunk past the floor, and **no two shapes overlap**. |
| D-166 | six **animation styles**, played as an overlay, written to nothing | locked | The wave 6c overlay (D-44) gains a **style** chosen in the properties-panel section (D-42): **focus** (what exists today), **beam** (a line of light travelling along each edge from the step to the next - the Animated Beam), **marching** (the edge's dashes move along it - the animated edge), **pipe** (marching, drawn as a pipe - D-171), **token** (a dot travels along the edge from one step to the next - React Flow's moving circle and JointJS's token), and **pulse** (the current step glows). The path a beam, dash or token follows is **the arrow's own geometry** - its points, taken from the element and converted with the library's own `sceneCoordsToViewportCoords` (D-34) - so it follows a curved or elbowed arrow and moves when the arrow moves. The clock is the `AnimationController` and never React state (D-63), nothing is written to the document or to `appState` (AC-51 still holds for every style; the camera flight of D-170 is the one thing that touches `appState`, it moves only the view, and it is off by default), and a style is **only** a way of drawing the overlay: the step order (D-43) is the same for all six. |
| D-167 | **interactive step-by-step**, and **play from here** | locked | The section gains **Present steps**: the flow does **not** loop; it shows the steps **reached so far** and dims the rest, and the **next** and **previous** controls - and the right and left arrow keys, which the overlay claims **only while it is presenting** - move one step. While presenting there is also **play from here**: it runs the **current step and everything downstream of it** once (the JointJS behaviour of clicking a node), in the step order, and returns to the canvas when it ends. Both are overlay-only, like every style, and leaving presentation restores the canvas exactly as it was. |
| D-168 | the two CodePen references, **read**: what is taken and what is not | locked | **Read, not copied**: both pens are somebody else's code (a Northwoods GoJS sample, and a pen by its author), so **none of their code, artwork or text is in this repository** - the wave takes the **ideas** and writes its own. **`GYeEVV` (Process Flow GoJS)** is a process diagram: each link is a **pipe** of three stacked strokes (black 5, grey 3, and a **white dashed line** `[10,10]`), and a 100 ms loop steps the dash offset by 2 so the flow runs along the pipe; it also has grid snapping, rotatable valve symbols, and links that route around nodes with a gap where two cross. **Taken**: the **pipe look** and its dash-offset flow (D-171). **Not taken**: GoJS itself, the valve and tank symbols (an engineering drawing's vocabulary, not a teacher's), the grid snap and the rotatable symbols - Excalidraw has its own snapping and a chart is made of its ordinary shapes (D-161). **`VjvGJM` (Animated Flow Chart)** is a click-through decision tree: clicking a **Yes** or **No** tweens the SVG `viewBox` for 1.5 s (ease-out) to the target step's bounds plus 75 units of padding - and for some branches through an **intermediate step first**, holding 1 s - and an **end** step has its outline **drawn on** stroke by stroke, then filled. **Taken**: the **camera that flies to the step** (D-170) and the **choose-your-path** flow with its **draw-on end** (D-172). **Not taken**: the artwork, the dark theme, the jQuery and TweenMax and DrawSVG plugins - the clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63), and nothing is added (D-169). |
| D-169 | nothing is added to the app but code | locked | No dependency, no font, no network request and no file larger than the code. The presets' example labels are **our own words**, and the shapes use the library's own fonts and colours, so the studio works with no internet. |
| D-170 | the **camera flies** to the step, and only the view moves | locked | While a flow is playing or being presented, the view **eases** to the current step: the target is the step's bounds (D-60, the container and its label together) **plus a padding** of 75 scene units - the number the reference pen uses - and the move takes **1.5 s** by default with an **ease-out** curve. The flight changes **only** `scrollX`, `scrollY` and `zoom` - the **view**, never an element and never the page structure - and the camera **stays on the page** (the page clamp, D-4, still applies, so a flight never leaves the page's rect and never goes below the zoom floor, AC-155). It is **off by default**, a switch in the section, because a teacher who is talking over a chart often wants the view to stay where it is. **Leaving** the flow restores the view to **exactly** where it was when the flow started. The tween's clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63). |
| D-171 | the **pipe** look | locked | A sixth style (D-166): each edge is drawn **as a pipe** - three strokes laid one over another, a **dark** outer stroke, a **lighter** inner stroke and a **dashed highlight** on top - and the highlight's **dash offset advances along the edge from the step to the next**, so the flow runs in the arrow's direction. The three strokes and the dash are drawn by the **overlay** along the arrow's own geometry (D-166); the arrow in the document keeps its own stroke and is not changed. The advance is **time-based** (a fixed speed in scene units per second), not frame-based, so a slow machine does not run the flow slower. |
| D-172 | **choose your path**, with a **draw-on** end | locked | In **present steps** (D-167) on a **decision** chart (D-163) the **branch shapes the teacher or the class picks**: while a question is the current step, its two branches are **clickable** in the overlay, and choosing one makes the **target of that branch** the next step - the flow follows the **choice**, not the step order. The step order (D-43) is still what `next` and `previous` follow when nobody chooses. When the next step is an **end** (a shape with no arrow leaving it), its outline is **drawn on** - the stroke grows from nothing to the whole outline over about 1.5 s - and then **filled**. Both are the overlay's: the end shape in the document is not changed, and leaving restores the canvas exactly (D-167). |
| D-173 | **resizing** a chart keeps its arrows on its shapes | locked | A chart (D-161) resized as a whole - the selection's handles, or the arrow keys with a modifier - keeps **every arrow bound at both ends** and **ending on the edge of its shape**: after the resize, no arrow end is off its shape, no binding names a shape that is gone, and a shape's `boundElements` still names its arrows (D-162's two-sided agreement still holds). The shapes' **text scales with them** and stays inside them. The fix is in the resize path that moves bound arrows, not a repair pass run after the fact, and a chart of the library's own presets is the case it is tested on. |
| D-174 | **lay this out** keeps the chart's scale, and can be **reset** | locked | The layout action (D-62) re-spaces a chart **without changing the size of its shapes or of their text**, so a chart laid out after it was resized keeps the size it had. The first time a chart is laid out, each shape's position is **remembered** on the shape (`customData.flow.home`, ordinary element data, so it saves, exports and undoes with the chart); a new action **reset layout** puts every shape back at its remembered position - arrows following, one undo step - and is offered only where there is something to reset. A shape that has been moved by hand since is **still reset**: reset means the remembered layout, not the last one. |
| D-175 | the animation's marks **scale with the chart** | locked | The beam's length, the dash lengths, the token's radius, the pipe's strokes and the pulse's glow are drawn in **scene units multiplied by a chart scale**: the chart's own text size (the median of its shapes' font sizes) divided by the preset's base size (24), kept between one half and four. A chart that is a hundred nodes at the floor font size gets **smaller marks** and a chart the teacher has resized up gets **larger ones**, and the same chart is drawn the same at any zoom. The scale is read from the elements each time the overlay draws, so it follows a resize at once. |
| D-176 | the **Flow tab fits any window** | locked | The tab is laid out as a **scrolling body with a footer that does not scroll**: the preset choices and the script take the room that is left and scroll inside it, and the **Insert chart** button and the message under it are **always visible and clickable** whatever the height of the window, including a window that is not full screen. The script box has a **minimum** height that still shows a few lines and no maximum that pushes the button off the panel. Nothing in the tab is reachable only by making the window bigger. |
| D-177 | the animation controls are a **floating bar** that stays with the chart | locked | The Flow animation section leaves the properties panel (D-42's section is **moved**, not copied): it becomes a **floating bar** drawn by the overlay layer, **anchored just below the chart** it controls, following it as the chart is moved or the view scrolls or zooms, and kept **inside the viewport** (pinned to the nearest edge when the chart's own edge is off screen). It carries everything the section had - play and stop, present steps, the two animation choices (D-178), the speed (D-179), the camera switch (D-170) and the loop switch - and **nothing else changes about what they do**. It is shown while a flow is selected (D-180) or playing, and not otherwise. |
| D-178 | **two animations**, one for the **nodes** and one for the **arrows**, combined | locked | The single style (D-166) becomes two choices that are **combined**: a **node animation** - none, focus, pulse - and an **arrow animation** - none, beam, marching, pipe, token. Any node animation goes with any arrow animation, and both are drawn at once over the same step order (D-43). The old single style still means what it did: a document or setting that holds a single style is read as the pair it was (focus is node focus with no arrow animation; pulse is node pulse; beam, marching, pipe and token are no node animation with that arrow animation). The choice is a **setting**, remembered between sessions like the others, and never written into the document (D-166). |
| D-179 | the **speed** of the flow is tunable | locked | The speed of the marks along the arrows - the pipe's and the marching dashes' flow, and the beam and the token - is a **setting** in scene units per second (before the chart scale of D-175), from **40 to 600**, **240** by default (D-171's fixed speed becomes the default). The control is a slider with its value shown, it changes the speed **while the animation is playing** without restarting it, and the advance stays **time-based**: the same distance in the same time at any frame rate, so doubling the speed doubles the distance covered in the same time. |
| D-180 | the animation is available **without selecting the whole chart** | locked | The floating bar appears when **any shape or arrow that belongs to a flow** is selected, and it controls the **whole connected flow** that element belongs to - the same graph the layout and the step order are built from - not just the selection. A flow that is **playing keeps playing** whatever is selected afterwards, and the overlay stays on until it is stopped; selecting something else does not hide it. The animation only ever **reads** the selection (D-166): nothing is written. |
| D-181 | the **presenter view shows the animation** | locked | The presenter window (D-64) draws the same flow animation as the editor. The editor **publishes the animation's state** - active or not, mode, node and arrow animations, speed, camera switch, the current step and the path, and the **ids of the steps** - over the existing presenter channel, in a message of its own that is sent **only when it changes** (and once when a presenter connects), and the presenter's overlay draws from it over **its own copy of the scene**, so a moved shape is followed there too. The presenter never writes an animation state back, and a presenter that is not showing a flow draws nothing and costs nothing. |
| D-182 | the board has an **edit view** and a **class view** | locked | The animation behaves differently in the two. In the **edit view** (the editor as it is) a flow animates only while the teacher plays it from the bar (D-177), exactly as in wave 7r. In the **class view** the animation is the picture, not an operation: charts that are **marked for class** (D-183) loop **by themselves**, with nothing selected and no bar. The class view is what a view-only editor already is - the editor in **view mode**, and the **presenter window** (D-64) - so there is no third mode and no new switch to forget: putting the editor in view mode is starting the class. The **published** exports (a GIF or a video) are files and a later wave; this row is the live board. |
| D-183 | **show in class**: a switch on the bar, saved with the chart | locked | The bar (D-177) gains **Show in class**. Switching it on **writes the flow's animation into the document** as ordinary element data - `customData.flow.class = { node, arrow, speed, stepMs, loop }` on **every step container of the connected flow** (D-180) - in **one undo step**, so it is saved, exported with the file, copied and pasted with the chart, and undone like anything else. The values are the bar's settings at that moment (D-178, D-179); the camera flight (D-170) is **not** part of it, because a class loop never moves the view. While the switch is on, **changing a setting on the bar writes through**, so what the teacher sees on the bar is what the class gets. Switching it off removes the entry. This is the one place an animation is written to the document, and it is a **choice the teacher makes**: playing from the bar still writes nothing (D-166). |
| D-184 | the **class loop** | locked | In the class view every marked flow **on the page being shown** plays by itself: its steps in the step order (D-43), one after another every `stepMs`, drawn with its stored node and arrow animations (D-166, D-178) at its stored speed (D-179) and its own chart scale (D-175), and **looping** unless its `loop` is off - then it plays once and **rests on its last step**. Flows are **independent**: each has its own clock, so several charts on a page animate at the same time, and a chart that is moved or resized in the editor is followed. A flow on another page is not drawn. The clock is the `AnimationController` (D-63), and **nothing runs when no marked flow is on the page**: the class view of a board with no marked flow costs nothing. |
| D-185 | **step through it**: a button on the chart | locked | In the class view each marked chart carries a small **Steps** button attached to the chart's corner and following it (placed with the library's own `sceneCoordsToViewportCoords`, D-34). Pressing it turns **that chart** from looping into **present steps** (D-167): the right and left arrows and the controls of D-167 move one step, choosing a branch works (D-172), and **Esc** or the close control returns the chart to **looping**. Only the chart that was pressed changes: the others keep looping. The arrow keys are taken only while a chart is being stepped (D-167) and are free the rest of the time. The button is not drawn in the edit view, and not in a **display-only** window (D-187). |
| D-186 | the **presenter window plays the class loop by itself** | locked | The presenter window (D-64) is always in the class view and plays every marked flow of the shown page from the **scene it already receives**: the stored settings travel in the elements, so **no new message** is needed and the loop runs even when the editor is idle. When the editor is stepping a chart (D-185), the editor's flow snapshot (D-181) reaches the presenter as before, and for **that chart** the presenter shows the mirror instead of its loop - a chart is never drawn twice - and when the stepping ends its loop resumes. The presenter writes nothing back (D-181). |
| D-187 | a **display-only** window | locked | The library gains an optional prop, `flowDisplayOnly`, for a window that shows the board and is not operated: it plays the class loop (D-184) and shows **no Steps button** (D-185) and no bar. The presenter window sets it. Without it, a view-only editor is the teacher's own view mode and has the Steps buttons. |
| D-188 | documents made before this, and charts not marked, are untouched | locked | A chart with no `customData.flow.class` never loops by itself in any view, so opening a document made before this wave changes nothing about it. The entry is read **defensively**: a missing entry, or one of the wrong type, is a flow that is **not marked**, and a partial entry takes the defaults for what it lacks. Marking a chart changes **nothing else** about its elements. |
| D-189 | the **colour of the highlight** is a setting | locked | The step highlight - the outline of **focus** and the glow of **pulse** (D-166, D-178) - takes a **colour**, `nodeColor`: a setting on the bar like the others (D-178), remembered between sessions, carried in the class entry (D-183) and in the presenter's snapshot (D-181), so the class and the projector show what the bar shows. The default is the editor's accent colour, which is what the highlight has always been. A value that is not a colour is read as the default (D-188's rule). It is the overlay's colour only: no element is changed. |
| D-190 | the colour of **what travels**, the colour of **the arrow**, and **hiding the arrow** | superseded by D-204 | Two more colours. `markColor` is the colour of what travels along an arrow - the beam, the token, the marching dashes and the pipe's highlight. `arrowColor` is the colour the **arrow itself** is drawn in while the animation plays, and it can be **hidden**: the overlay then paints the arrow in the **page's background colour**, so only what travels along it is seen. This is drawn by the overlay **over** the arrow, so the arrow in the document is **not changed** and is back the moment the animation stops (D-166). The defaults are what is drawn today: the marks in the accent colour, the pipe with its own dark and light strokes, and nothing painted over the arrow. Same carrying rules as D-189. |
| D-191 | highlight **only the text** | superseded by D-201 | A setting, `nodeTarget`: **shape** (the whole node - what it is now) or **text** (only the label inside it). With **text** the highlight is drawn on the label's own bounds and the shape is left as it is. A node with no label has no text to highlight and takes the shape. Same carrying rules as D-189. |
| D-192 | the highlight has the **node's own shape** | locked | The highlight of a step - the focus outline, the pulse glow, the dimming of steps not reached (D-167) and the branches that can be chosen (D-172) - is drawn in the shape of the node it is on, not in a rectangle: a **rectangle** with its own corner roundness, an **ellipse** as an ellipse, a **diamond** as a diamond, and a **rotated** node at its own angle. The shape is read from the container element (its type, its roundness and its angle), so a node that is changed in the editor is followed. A node the highlight has no outline for (a free-form shape, an image) takes the rectangle of its bounds. |
| D-193 | the editor can **preview the class loop** | locked | The bar gains **Preview class loop**: while it is on, the class loop (D-184) plays in the **edit view** too, for every marked chart on the page - so ticking **Show in class** has a visible effect, and untick or undo takes the animation away together with the mark. Ticking **Show in class** turns the preview on, once. The preview is a setting of this editor only: it is **not saved** with the document and **not sent** to the presenter. It never takes the pointer and has no Steps button (D-185), so the board can be edited while it plays. |
| D-194 | a flow that is done **holds its last step** | locked | A flow whose loop is off plays once and **holds its last step** (D-184): it stays on the step it ended on, **highlight included**, so the board shows where the flow ended. That is what it does today and it does not change. A looping flow never ends. |
| D-195 | the look **travels** with the flow | locked | The class entry (D-183) and the presenter's snapshot (D-181) carry `nodeColor`, `markColor`, `arrowColor` (or hidden) and `nodeTarget` with the rest, so the class view, the presenter, the mirror and a pasted chart show the look the bar shows. An entry made before this wave has none of them and takes the defaults (D-188): its look is exactly what it was. |
| D-196 | the controls for the look are a **Look** section of the bar | locked | The bar (D-177) gains a **Look** section, closed until it is opened, so the bar stays as small as it was: a colour for the highlight, a colour for what travels, a colour for the arrow with **Hide arrow** beside it, a choice of **whole shape** or **text only**, and **Preview class loop** (D-193). Opening it makes the bar taller; the bar still stays **inside the viewport** and is still placed below the chart (D-177). While **Show in class** is on, a change in the Look section writes through to the chart like any other bar setting (D-183). |
| D-197 | the bar is a **movable pill** | locked | Like the toolbar and the properties panel, the bar has a **move grip** and a **collapse** button. Dragging the grip moves the bar anywhere in the editor: it is kept **inside the viewport** (with the margin of D-177) and it **snaps** to an edge it is dropped near. Until the teacher moves it, the bar sits below the chart and follows it (D-177); once moved it **stays where it was put**, whatever the chart or the view does, and **double-clicking the grip** sends it back to following the chart. Where it was put is remembered between sessions, as a place in the window and not in the document - as the other pills are. A remembered place that is not usable (missing, off the window, wrong type) is ignored and the bar follows the chart. |
| D-198 | the bar **collapses into a pill** | locked | The collapse button turns the bar into a small **pill** where the bar was, and pressing the pill opens the bar again. Collapsing changes only what is shown: a flow that is playing keeps playing, and the settings are untouched. A collapsed bar is remembered like the place (D-197), and the pill is movable by the same grip rule. |
| D-199 | moving the bar does not touch the canvas | locked | Pressing or dragging the grip or the pill is **not a click on the canvas**: nothing is deselected, no element is moved, and the flow the bar controls stays the same. The bar's own controls work as before, and after a **window resize** the bar is brought back inside the viewport. |
| D-207 | the colours are chosen from a **swatch pad** | locked | Each colour in the Look section (D-196) - the shape and the text (D-209), what travels and the arrow (D-204) - is chosen from a **swatch pad** in the editor's own style instead of a bare colour input: a row of swatches - **default** (the accent colour; choosing it clears the colour, D-189), then the **neon** red, orange, yellow, green, cyan, blue, purple and pink (#ff3131, #ff9100, #fff01f, #39ff14, #00f0ff, #3d7bff, #bc13fe and #ff2bd6), then white, black and grey - with the **current choice ringed**, and a last swatch that opens a **custom** colour (the browser's own picker) for anything else. A swatch is a button with a name, the pad is one radio group and can be reached and used from the keyboard, and what a swatch sets is a hex colour like any other, so it is saved, carried and read exactly as D-189 and D-195 say. A custom colour that is the same as a swatch rings that swatch. |
| D-201 | what is highlighted: **five choices** | locked | `nodeTarget` (D-191) has **five** values - what the highlight is put on: **shape** (the node's outline: what the highlight is today), **shape + text box** (the outline, and a rectangle on the label's own box), **shape + text** (the outline, and the label's letters), **text box** (a rectangle on the label's box and nothing else: what D-191's "text only" drew) and **text** (only the label's letters, D-202). The bar's _Highlight on_ menu offers them in that order, named _Shape_, _Shape + text box_, _Shape + text_, _Text box_ and _Text_. A node with no label has no text and takes the **shape** for every choice that names a text. A value stored before this wave is read as what it drew: _shape_ stays, and the old _text_ is read as **text box**. Same carrying rules as D-189 and D-195. |
| D-202 | the label's **letters** are highlighted as letters | locked | **Text** and **shape + text** highlight the label's **letters**: the overlay draws the label's text again, **over** the canvas's own, in the highlight colour - in the label's own font, size, line height, alignment and angle, at the label's own place, with the glow of pulse or the lift of focus - so that the letters on the page are the letters that light up, and **no rectangle** is drawn round them. It is the overlay's copy: the label in the document is not changed. Only the label of the step that is current lights up. |
| D-203 | the labels of **arrows** are above the animation | locked | A label on an **arrow** (the yes and no of a decision, D-163) is drawn **above** whatever is animated along that arrow - the beam, the token, the dashes, the pipe - and above the highlight: the overlay draws each arrow label again over the marks, in the label's own font, size, colour and alignment, at its own place, as D-202 does for a node's letters. It is drawn in every view that animates (the editor, the class view, the preview, the presenter) and only while something is animated; it is the overlay's copy and the label in the document is not changed. |
| D-204 | **hiding** and **recolouring** the arrow are the canvas's own drawing | locked | Replaces D-190. **Hide arrow** and the arrow's colour stop being a cover painted over the arrow and become **the canvas's own drawing**: while a flow is animated (played or stepped from the bar, or looping in the class view or the preview) the editor's canvas **does not draw** a hidden arrow - nothing is over it, so nothing is left at its ends or on a node's border - and draws a recoloured arrow **in its colour**. The list the canvas is given to draw is built in one place, the renderer, and that is where the animation's state is applied: for each arrow of an animated flow the canvas is given the arrow **without**, or a **copy** with its stroke colour changed. A copy is kept and reused, so it is not rebuilt each frame, and it is made again when its arrow changes. The arrow in the document and the elements the editor holds are **never changed**; an exported image or file shows the arrow as it is drawn; selection and hit-testing see the arrow as before; and the labels bound to an arrow stay (D-203). The moment the animation stops the arrow is drawn again. The colour of **what travels** (the beam, the token, the dashes and the pipe's highlight) is the overlay's, as D-190 said, and the pipe's strokes are made from it (D-210). D-190's cover - the stroke and the head circle - is removed. |
| D-205 | every window does the same | locked | The presenter window (D-64) and the class view do all of this the same way: the presenter's canvas is its own, so it applies D-204 to its own drawing - from the class entry (D-195) for the flows it loops, and from the snapshot (D-181) for the flow it mirrors - and the letters (D-202) and the arrow labels (D-203) are drawn in its overlay. A window with nothing animated draws everything as it is. |
| D-206 | the colours of the whole animation come in **presets** | locked | The Look section (D-196) opens with a row of **colour presets**. Each sets the **shape** colour, the **text** colour (D-209) and the colour of **what travels** (D-204) **together**, and turns **glow** (D-208) on, so the colours of the whole animation change in one press, with nothing to customise; each colour can still be changed afterwards. There are eight: **Classic** (the accent colour, no glow - the default), **Cyan and pink**, **Green and purple**, **Yellow and blue**, **Orange and cyan**, **Pink and lime**, **Fire and ice** and **High contrast**. The colours are **neon**: saturated, bright colours that read as light and not as paint. A neon yellow or green cannot be seen on a white page at any glow, so each preset has two variants - the **pure neon** for a **dark page**, and a **deep neon** for a **light page**, the most vivid colour that still reaches 3 : 1 contrast against white, the WCAG 2.1 bound for graphics (SC 1.4.11) - and the variant that reads on the **current page background** is the one applied. **Every colour of every preset is held to at least 3 : 1 against the page it is for**, and the shape colour and the colour of what travels differ in **hue by at least 60 degrees**, so the two never blur into one. A preset sets nothing but those colours and the glow (the arrow's colour, Hide arrow, the target and the speed are left as they are), writes through to a marked chart like any other setting (D-183), and shows as **chosen** while its colours are the ones in use. **Classic** clears the colours and the glow, which is the default (D-189). |
| D-208 | **glow**: the highlight as a **neon tube** | locked | **Glow** is a setting of the look that makes the highlight, and what travels, look like a **neon tube** - the neon of a sign - and not like paint: a thin, bright **core**, which is the colour lightened toward white, with a **halo** round it in the colour itself, made of several soft shadows of growing reach so that it blooms, and **no fill**. With glow on, a highlighted node is a glowing outline and nothing else - not a tinted box - and a text box is a glowing rectangle. It applies to the outline of a node, the rectangle on a label's box, the label's **letters** (a hot core in the letters themselves, with the halo as a soft shadow round them) and to the beam, the token, the dashes and the pipe. It is a switch in the Look section (_Neon glow_), **off** by default, so the default look is what it has always been; **on** in every preset but Classic (D-206); remembered between sessions; and carried in the class entry (D-183, D-195) and the presenter's snapshot (D-181) like the colours. An entry from before it has none and takes the default (D-188). The glow takes the colour of what it surrounds - the shape's colour round a shape, the text colour (D-209) round a text box and letters, the animation colour round what travels - and it is the overlay's: it is drawn with strokes, drop shadows and text shadows, so nothing is added to the document. |
| D-209 | the highlight's **shape** and **text** are coloured apart | locked | The highlight colour (D-189) is **bifurcated**: the **shape** colour (`nodeColor`, as before) is the colour of the node's outline, and a new **text** colour (`textColor`) is the colour of the rectangle on a label's box (D-201) and of its letters (D-202). With glow on (D-208) these colours are the **neon highlight only**: they are the colour of the tube and of its halo, and nothing else on the node is coloured by them - there is no fill. Until the text colour is set it **follows** the shape colour, so nothing changes for a look that never uses it. The Look section's _Highlight_ row becomes two, _Shape_ and _Text_, each its own swatch pad (D-207). The presets set both (D-206), and the text colour is validated, remembered, written to the class entry (D-183, D-195) and sent to the presenter (D-181) as the shape colour is; an entry from before it has none, which is "follow the shape" (D-188). |
| D-210 | the **pipe** takes the animation colour | locked | While the animation colour (D-204) is set, the pipe's three strokes (D-171) are made from it: the **outer** stroke a darker shade of it (60 % of the way to black), the **body** the colour itself, and the highlight that runs along it a lighter tint (80 % of the way to white); and the pipe glows (D-208) when glow is on. While it is not set the pipe is what it has always been - dark, grey and white. The shades are computed from the colour and not chosen from a list, so every swatch, preset and custom colour has a pipe. |
| D-211 | what the overlay draws **lines up** with the canvas | locked | A label's **letters** (D-202) and the arrow labels (D-203) are drawn as **SVG text with the canvas's own rule** - the same font, size and line height, and the **same baseline offset** the canvas uses for each line - so a letter lands on the letter under it; a **text box** or **letters** highlight is lifted by _focus_ about **its own centre**, not about the centre of the whole node, so it grows in place instead of drifting off the text it belongs to; and the outline of a node is drawn **inside** the node's box, so that its stroke does not reach past the edge. |
| D-212 | a change of the **view alone** sends the view alone | locked | While nothing in the scene changes - the camera flight of D-170, a scroll, a zoom - the editor sends a **camera** message, carrying the view (the same description the scene message carries), at the screen's own rate, instead of the whole scene; the scene message goes only when the elements, the files or the pages change, and once when a presenter connects (D-181). The presenter applies a camera message by moving its **own view** and does **nothing else**: it does not replace its elements or draw them again, so a flight is as smooth there as in the editor. |
| D-213 | a label is **never seen twice** | locked | While the overlay draws a label again - the letters of a lit label (D-202) and the label of an arrow above the animation (D-203) - the editor's canvas does **not** draw that label: the list the canvas is given (D-204) is built without it. So there is one set of letters on the screen, never two, wherever the chart is moved, scrolled or zoomed, and a copy that sits a pixel away from the original can no longer be seen as a second set. The label in the document is not changed, an exported image shows it, and the canvas draws it again the moment the overlay stops drawing it. It holds for the class loop, the preview, the flow that is played or stepped, and the presenter (D-205). |
| D-214 | leaving the animation is **clear** | locked | Three things that were easy to mix up are told apart. **Minimise** (the close button the toolbox has, on the bar, D-198) only turns the bar into a pill, and the animation goes on. **Stop** ends the animation that is played or stepped, and switches off the preview of the class loop (D-193). And the way out is always in view: the pill says what is running - _playing_, _stepping_ or _preview_ - and carries a **Stop** button, so the animation can be ended without opening the bar; the bar has the same Stop; and **Esc** exits whatever is running - a played flow, the step mode, the preview - in one press, wherever the focus is, unless text is being edited or another mode already uses Esc. Nothing is left running with no visible way to end it. The class view in view mode (D-182) is left by leaving view mode, as before. |
| D-215 | the **laser pointer** is a neon tube | locked | The laser pointer (the tool on the key K) is drawn as a **neon tube** (D-208): a thin, bright **core** - the laser's colour lightened toward white - laid over a **halo** in the laser's own colour, made of several soft shadows of growing reach, so the trail blooms like the red neon of a sign. It is the same trail it was: the same path, the same fade-out and the same length, only drawn as two strokes and a glow. It is the look of the tool and not a setting, and it holds for every laser the editor draws - the teacher's own, a collaborator's in their colour, and the one the presenter window mirrors - and for no other trail (the eraser's and the lasso's are not changed). |
| D-216 | the **emoji bar** has the toolbox's **move** and **close** | locked | The emoji and GIF bar (D-159) becomes one of the editor's **docks**, with the toolbox's own controls: a **move grip** - drag it to put the bar anywhere, and it snaps to an edge it is dropped near - and a **close** button that turns the bar into a **pill** on the nearest edge, which brings it back when it is pressed. Its place and its collapsed state are remembered between sessions, like the toolbox's. It keeps what it had: its tabs, its search, and the drag of an emoji or a GIF onto the canvas (D-157 to D-160). The page bar is not changed. |

| AC-195 | every preset builds a **connected, bound** chart | `packages/excalidraw/tests/flowPresets.test.ts` | For each of the seven presets and several label counts (one, two, many): every arrow's two bindings name shapes **in the result**, every shape's `boundElements` names the arrow back, every shape's label is bound text, ids are unique, the same seed gives the same chart, and the result touches no DOM. Tests: `AC-195 every preset builds bound arrows with both sides agreeing`, `AC-195 every shape carries its label as bound text`, `AC-195 ids are unique and the seed makes the chart repeatable`, `AC-195 a loop returns to its first step`, `AC-195 the decision rejoins`, `AC-195 the tiers stack and the hub is in the middle`. |

Tests: `AC-196 blank lines are ignored and labels are trimmed`, `AC-196 a tier line splits into its items`, `AC-196 the first line of an integration is the hub`, `AC-196 a question takes the next two lines as its branches`, `AC-196 an edge line creates each node once`, `AC-196 an empty script builds nothing and says so`. |

Tests: `AC-197 every shape is inside the page`, `AC-197 no two shapes overlap`, `AC-197 the text never goes below the floor`, `AC-197 a script too big for the floor is reported`. |

Tests: `AC-199 the beam travels the arrow's path`, `AC-199 the token travels the arrow's path`, `AC-199 the marching dashes follow the arrow`, `AC-199 the pulse is on the current step`, `AC-199 a curved arrow is followed, and a moved arrow too`, `AC-199 no style writes into the document`, `AC-199 the overlay is above the canvases it draws on`, `AC-199 the marks are where the arrows are, on the canvas's own pixels`. |

Tests: `AC-200 presenting reveals one step at a time`, `AC-200 next and previous stop at the ends`, `AC-200 the arrow keys are only claimed while presenting`, `AC-200 play from here runs the downstream steps in order`, `AC-200 leaving restores the canvas`. |

Tests: `AC-201 the pipe is three strokes on the arrow's path`, `AC-201 the dash runs toward the arrow's end`, `AC-201 the speed does not depend on the frame rate`, `AC-201 the arrow in the document is not changed`. |
